### Distributed LLMs and Multimodal Large Language Models: A Survey on Advances, Challenges, and Future Directions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16585v1)
- **Authors**: Hadi Amini, Md Jueal Mia, Yasaman Saadati, Ahmed Imteaj, Seyedsina Nabavirazavi, Urmish Thakker, Md Zarif Hossain, Awal Ahmed Fime, S. S. Iyengar
- **Abstract**: Language models (LMs) are machine learning models designed to predict linguistic patterns by estimating the probability of word sequences based on large-scale datasets, such as text. LMs have a wide range of applications in natural language processing (NLP) tasks, including autocomplete and machine translation. Although larger datasets typically enhance LM performance, scalability remains a challenge due to constraints in computational power and resources. Distributed computing strategies offer essential solutions for improving scalability and managing the growing computational demand. Further, the use of sensitive datasets in training and deployment raises significant privacy concerns. Recent research has focused on developing decentralized techniques to enable distributed training and inference while utilizing diverse computational resources and enabling edge AI. This paper presents a survey on distributed solutions for various LMs, including large language models (LLMs), vision language models (VLMs), multimodal LLMs (MLLMs), and small language models (SLMs). While LLMs focus on processing and generating text, MLLMs are designed to handle multiple modalities of data (e.g., text, images, and audio) and to integrate them for broader applications. To this end, this paper reviews key advancements across the MLLM pipeline, including distributed training, inference, fine-tuning, and deployment, while also identifying the contributions, limitations, and future areas of improvement. Further, it categorizes the literature based on six primary focus areas of decentralization. Our analysis describes gaps in current methodologies for enabling distributed solutions for LMs and outline future research directions, emphasizing the need for novel solutions to enhance the robustness and applicability of distributed LMs.

### Transformer-based Wireless Symbol Detection Over Fading Channels
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16594v1)
- **Authors**: Li Fan, Jing Yang, Cong Shen
- **Abstract**: Pre-trained Transformers, through in-context learning (ICL), have demonstrated exceptional capabilities to adapt to new tasks using example prompts without model update. Transformer-based wireless receivers, where prompts consist of the pilot data in the form of transmitted and received signal pairs, have shown high detection accuracy when pilot data are abundant. However, pilot information is often costly and limited in practice. In this work, we propose the DEcision Feedback INcontExt Detection (DEFINED) solution as a new wireless receiver design, which bypasses channel estimation and directly performs symbol detection using the (sometimes extremely) limited pilot data. The key innovation in DEFINED is the proposed decision feedback mechanism in ICL, where we sequentially incorporate the detected symbols into the prompts as pseudo-labels to improve the detection for subsequent symbols. Furthermore, we proposed another detection method where we combine ICL with Semi-Supervised Learning (SSL) to extract information from both labeled and unlabeled data during inference, thus avoiding the errors propagated during the decision feedback process of the original DEFINED. Extensive experiments across a broad range of wireless communication settings demonstrate that a small Transformer trained with DEFINED or IC-SSL achieves significant performance improvements over conventional methods, in some cases only needing a single pilot pair to achieve similar performance of the latter with more than 4 pilot pairs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel method called Decision Feedback IN-Context Detection (DEFINED) for wireless symbol detection over fading channels.  It leverages pre-trained Transformers and in-context learning (ICL) to bypass explicit channel estimation.  DEFINED sequentially incorporates detected symbols into the prompt as pseudo-labels (decision feedback) to improve subsequent symbol detection. An additional detection method combines ICL with Semi-Supervised Learning (SSL) to extract information from both labeled and unlabeled data, mitigating error propagation from the decision feedback process. The paper demonstrates, through extensive experiments, that a small Transformer trained with DEFINED or IC-SSL achieves performance improvements over traditional methods, even with very limited pilot data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several key areas:

    *   *Decision Feedback in ICL:* The core idea of incorporating decision feedback within the ICL framework for symbol detection is innovative. It addresses a critical limitation of standard ICL-based receivers, which require substantial pilot data. Using previously decoded symbols as pseudo-labels to augment the context is a significant step.
    *   *IC-SSL:* Formulating the detection problem as a semi-supervised learning problem within the ICL context (IC-SSL) is another novel aspect. This addresses the challenge of imperfect decision feedback by leveraging unlabeled data (received signals without known transmitted symbols) to refine the model's understanding of the channel characteristics.
    *   *Mixture Training Process:* The specifically designed training process for DEFINED which involves pre-training on clean data, followed by a tailored decision-feedback based fine-tuning, contributes to adapting the model to practical scenarios with limited pilots while ensuring robustness in clean conditions.
    *   *End-to-End Multi-Class Approach:* Formulating the problem as a classification problem directly optimizing for Symbol Error Rate (SER), in contrast to the MSE-based regression approach common in prior works.
*   **Significance:**

    *   *Practical Applicability:* The approach has strong practical implications, particularly in scenarios where pilot data is scarce or costly to acquire. The ability to achieve comparable or better performance with fewer pilots translates directly to improved spectral efficiency and reduced overhead in wireless communication systems.
    *   *Generalization Ability:* The experimental results suggest that the DEFINED method generalizes well to different modulation schemes, channel conditions, and even mismatched channel distributions. This robustness is crucial for real-world deployments, where the environment may vary significantly from the training data.
    *   *Lightweight Model:* The use of a relatively small Transformer model (0.42M parameters) is important. This makes the approach suitable for implementation on resource-constrained devices or edge deployments, something often overlooked by approaches involving larger models.
    *   *Broader Implications:* IC-SSL contributes significantly and may have broader implications in wireless communication. Utilizing unlabeled data more effectively is a promising direction for future research in areas such as channel estimation and interference mitigation, where large amounts of unlabeled data are readily available.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   *Clear Problem Formulation:* The paper clearly defines the problem of symbol detection with limited pilot data and provides a well-motivated rationale for the proposed approach.
    *   *Thorough Experimental Evaluation:* The experimental results are extensive and cover a wide range of scenarios, including different modulation schemes, SNR levels, SISO/MIMO systems, and channel distributions. The comparison against relevant baselines provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed methods.
    *   *Well-Structured and Clearly Written:* The paper is well-organized and written in a clear and concise manner, making it easy to understand the key ideas and results.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   *Theoretical Analysis:* The paper could benefit from a more in-depth theoretical analysis of why the proposed methods work. While the experimental results are compelling, a theoretical justification would strengthen the contribution. For example, analysis of the bias and variance introduced by the pseudo-labels in the DEFINED approach would be valuable. While there is a reference to prior ICL theoretical analysis, this is not tightly coupled with the architecture proposed in the paper.
    *   *Complexity Analysis:* While the small size of the transformer is highlighted, a more detailed analysis of the computational complexity of DEFINED and IC-SSL, particularly during inference, would be useful for practical implementation considerations.  A comparison of the computational load compared to a traditional channel estimation plus symbol detection chain would be useful.
    *   *Overlapping Blocks of symbols for Non-coherent scenarios:* The workaround of using overlapping blocks for resolving Phase ambiguity is only used on SISO BPSK and QPSK scenarios. In addition to being difficult to implement in a blind scenario, as mentioned in the paper, it is difficult to apply the same technique to higher dimensional schemes such as 16QAM and 64QAM.
    *   *Limited discussion about implementation challenges:* It would be good to include some more discussion on the limitations of the approach in real-world channels where channel statistics vary in time (i.e., outside a block-fading channel).

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the design of future wireless receivers. The DEFINED and IC-SSL frameworks offer a promising approach to symbol detection in challenging environments with limited pilot data, and could inspire further research in the application of in-context learning and semi-supervised learning to other wireless communication problems.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a genuinely novel and practically significant contribution to the field of wireless communication. The proposed DEFINED and IC-SSL methods offer a compelling alternative to traditional symbol detection techniques, particularly in scenarios where pilot data is limited. The extensive experimental results provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of the approach. However, the lack of theoretical analysis, complexity analysis, limited scenarios of usage of non-coherent techniques, and implementation challenges prevent a higher score. Despite these limitations, the paper has the potential to significantly impact the design of future wireless receivers and inspire further research in the application of in-context learning and semi-supervised learning to wireless communication systems.

- **Classification**: cs.IT
- **Score**: 8/10

### A Recipe for Generating 3D Worlds From a Single Image
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16611v1)
- **Authors**: Katja Schwarz, Denys Rozumnyi, Samuel Rota Bulò, Lorenzo Porzi, Peter Kontschieder
- **Abstract**: We introduce a recipe for generating immersive 3D worlds from a single image by framing the task as an in-context learning problem for 2D inpainting models. This approach requires minimal training and uses existing generative models. Our process involves two steps: generating coherent panoramas using a pre-trained diffusion model and lifting these into 3D with a metric depth estimator. We then fill unobserved regions by conditioning the inpainting model on rendered point clouds, requiring minimal fine-tuning. Tested on both synthetic and real images, our method produces high-quality 3D environments suitable for VR display. By explicitly modeling the 3D structure of the generated environment from the start, our approach consistently outperforms state-of-the-art, video synthesis-based methods along multiple quantitative image quality metrics. Project Page: https://katjaschwarz.github.io/worlds/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel pipeline for generating immersive 3D worlds from a single image, specifically targeted for VR experiences. The approach decomposes the problem into two main stages: 2D panorama synthesis and 3D lifting. Panorama synthesis is framed as an in-context learning problem for existing inpainting models, achieving coherent panoramas without additional training using a vision-language model for prompting. 3D lifting involves using metric depth estimation on rendered images from the panorama, followed by point cloud-conditioned inpainting to fill occluded areas. The resulting 3D structure is represented using Gaussian Splatting, augmented with a distortion correction mechanism, leading to high-quality and detailed VR environments. The paper shows quantitative and qualitative improvements over state-of-the-art methods on both synthetic and real-world images.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The decomposition of the single image to 3D world generation into panorama synthesis and point cloud-conditioned inpainting is a notable architectural contribution. While each component (inpainting models, depth estimation, Gaussian Splatting) isn't entirely novel, their clever combination and the framing of panorama synthesis as in-context learning provides a good amount of innovation. The forward-backward warping strategy for point cloud inpainting seems more like an implementation trick rather than a significant methodological breakthrough. The distortion correction mechanism for Gaussian Splatting also feels like an incremental improvement.

* **Significance:**  Generating 3D environments from single images has practical applications in VR/AR content creation, gaming, and entertainment. The paper directly addresses this goal, making its application significance evident. The improvements demonstrated over existing methods (WonderJourney, DimensionX) are substantial, indicating a real step forward in quality and consistency of generated 3D scenes. The use of existing generative models to minimize training and allow easier generalization to new styles enhances the practical impact.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem decomposition:** Breaking down the complex task into manageable sub-problems is well-justified and allows leveraging specialized techniques.
    *   **Training-free panorama generation:** The in-context learning approach eliminates the need for training a dedicated panorama synthesis model, which is a significant advantage.
    *   **Strong results:** The qualitative and quantitative results demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach in generating high-quality and coherent 3D environments.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper includes thorough comparisons with state-of-the-art methods and ablation studies.
    *   **Explicit focus on VR application:** Design choices are well-motivated by the target VR experience.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on pre-trained models:** While leveraging pre-trained models is efficient, the performance of the pipeline is limited by the capabilities of these models (inpainting, depth estimation). Specific choices are made for legal reasons, which might limit future research.
    *   **Limited navigable area:**  The navigable area within the generated 3D world is relatively small (2-meter cube), which could be a limitation for some VR applications.
    *   **Backside generation limitations:** The pipeline struggles with generating the backsides of occluded areas, leaving room for improvement.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the field by demonstrating a practical and effective approach for single image to 3D world generation. The decomposition strategy and the in-context learning approach for panorama synthesis could inspire further research in this area. The integration of existing generative models with 3D reconstruction techniques is a promising direction.

*   **Justification:** The paper offers a clear and technically sound approach to an important problem, with compelling results. The novelty lies in the architectural decomposition and the intelligent use of in-context learning to generate coherent panoramas. While individual components aren't entirely novel, their synergistic combination is innovative and impactful.  The limitations are acknowledged, providing avenues for future research. Overall, it's a solid contribution that advances the state-of-the-art in single image to 3D scene generation.

### Leveraging Large Language Models for Explainable Activity Recognition in Smart Homes: A Critical Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16622v1)
- **Authors**: Michele Fiori, Gabriele Civitarese, Priyankar Choudhary, Claudio Bettini
- **Abstract**: Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI) aims to uncover the inner reasoning of machine learning models. In IoT systems, XAI improves the transparency of models processing sensor data from multiple heterogeneous devices, ensuring end-users understand and trust their outputs. Among the many applications, XAI has also been applied to sensor-based Activities of Daily Living (ADLs) recognition in smart homes. Existing approaches highlight which sensor events are most important for each predicted activity, using simple rules to convert these events into natural language explanations for non-expert users. However, these methods produce rigid explanations lacking natural language flexibility and are not scalable. With the recent rise of Large Language Models (LLMs), it is worth exploring whether they can enhance explanation generation, considering their proven knowledge of human activities. This paper investigates potential approaches to combine XAI and LLMs for sensor-based ADL recognition. We evaluate if LLMs can be used: a) as explainable zero-shot ADL recognition models, avoiding costly labeled data collection, and b) to automate the generation of explanations for existing data-driven XAI approaches when training data is available and the goal is higher recognition rates. Our critical evaluation provides insights into the benefits and challenges of using LLMs for explainable ADL recognition.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Leveraging Large Language Models for Explainable Activity Recognition in Smart Homes: A Critical Evaluation" explores the potential of using Large Language Models (LLMs) for explainable activity recognition (XAR) in smart homes. It investigates two main approaches: (1) using LLMs as zero-shot ADL recognition models that also provide explanations (LLMe2e) and (2) using LLMs to generate natural language explanations for existing data-driven XAR approaches (LLMExplainer). The authors evaluate these methods on two public datasets, comparing their performance with baseline approaches like DeXAR and ADL-LLM.  They also conduct user surveys to assess the quality of the generated explanations.  The paper identifies key drawbacks and risks associated with using LLMs in XAR, including over-reliance, hallucinations, cost, and privacy issues, providing a critical perspective on their application.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a timely and relevant problem. While LLMs have been explored for HAR, the focus on *explainability* specifically, especially with a critical lens on potential drawbacks, provides a valuable and novel contribution.  Exploring both zero-shot and explanation generation scenarios is a strong point. The introduction of LLMe2e and LLMExplainer are innovative contributions that demonstrate two unique application contexts.
    *   **Sound Methodology:** The experimental setup is well-defined, using established datasets and appropriate baseline comparisons.  The user surveys provide valuable insights into the perceived quality of the explanations. The detailed discussion of experimental setup and data pre-processing contributes to the reliability and replicability of the results.
    *   **Important Insights:** The paper provides a balanced and critical evaluation of LLMs for XAR. The identification of the "over-reliance" issue and the discussion of potential mitigation strategies are particularly insightful. The discussion of hallucinations and their impact on the trustworthiness of explanations is crucial.
    *   **Clarity and Organization:** The paper is well-written and clearly structured, making it easy to follow the methodology, results, and discussion.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Dataset Diversity:** While the chosen datasets are established, expanding the evaluation to a wider range of smart home environments and activities could strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Dependency on OpenAI's `gpt-4`:** The exclusive use of OpenAI's `gpt-4` model may limit the transferability and scalability of the findings, as different LLMs may exhibit varying levels of performance and cost-effectiveness.
    *   **Over-Reliance Mitigation Strategies:** The mitigation strategies are promising but remain largely theoretical. Implementing and empirically evaluating these strategies would significantly improve the paper's practical impact. Further exploration and quantitative validation of these strategies are needed.
    *   **Generalization of User Survey:** Although the survey involved a substantial number of participants, the homogeneity of the survey population (e.g., predominantly tech-savvy individuals) could limit the generalizability of the findings to broader user demographics.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper contributes to the growing body of research on XAI in pervasive computing, specifically focusing on the challenges and opportunities presented by LLMs.
    *   The findings have implications for the design and development of future smart home systems that aim to provide transparent and trustworthy activity recognition capabilities.
    *   The critical evaluation of LLMs for XAR raises important questions about the ethical considerations and potential unintended consequences of using these models in real-world applications.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by providing a timely and critical evaluation of LLMs for explainable activity recognition in smart homes. The paper's novelty lies in its focus on explainability and the explicit discussion of drawbacks and risks, which are often overlooked in the excitement surrounding LLMs. Despite some limitations in dataset diversity and the lack of empirical validation for mitigation strategies, the paper offers valuable insights and raises important questions for future research. The clear structure, sound methodology, and important insights make it a noteworthy contribution to the area of XAI for pervasive computing.

### TriTex: Learning Texture from a Single Mesh via Triplane Semantic Features
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16630v1)
- **Authors**: Dana Cohen-Bar, Daniel Cohen-Or, Gal Chechik, Yoni Kasten
- **Abstract**: As 3D content creation continues to grow, transferring semantic textures between 3D meshes remains a significant challenge in computer graphics. While recent methods leverage text-to-image diffusion models for texturing, they often struggle to preserve the appearance of the source texture during texture transfer. We present \ourmethod, a novel approach that learns a volumetric texture field from a single textured mesh by mapping semantic features to surface colors. Using an efficient triplane-based architecture, our method enables semantic-aware texture transfer to a novel target mesh. Despite training on just one example, it generalizes effectively to diverse shapes within the same category. Extensive evaluation on our newly created benchmark dataset shows that \ourmethod{} achieves superior texture transfer quality and fast inference times compared to existing methods. Our approach advances single-example texture transfer, providing a practical solution for maintaining visual coherence across related 3D models in applications like game development and simulation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "TriTex: Learning Texture from a Single Mesh via Triplane Semantic Features" introduces a novel method for transferring textures from a single textured 3D mesh to other meshes while preserving semantic relationships. The approach, called TRITEX, learns a volumetric texture field by mapping semantic features to surface colors using a triplane-based architecture.  The method uses pre-trained semantic features (Diff3F), projects them onto triplanes, and processes them through a mapping network to generate the final triplane features. This allows for semantic-aware texture transfer to new target meshes, even with significant shape variations, despite training on only a single example. The authors demonstrate superior texture transfer quality and fast inference times compared to existing methods on a newly created benchmark dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the combination of existing techniques (triplane representation, pre-trained semantic features, convolutional processing) for a specific task: single-example texture transfer with semantic awareness. While the individual components are not entirely new, the way they are integrated and applied to this particular problem is original. The use of Diff3F features for encoding 3D semantic information directly into the triplane representation for texture mapping is a key innovation. This enables the model to generalize to new shapes within the same category from a single training instance. The claim of generalization is supported by quantitative and qualitative results.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant challenge in 3D content creation: achieving visual consistency across related 3D models. Single-example texture transfer offers a practical solution for applications like game development and simulation where it might be difficult or expensive to acquire large datasets. While the results look promising, the method is inherently limited by the information contained in the single source mesh and might struggle with generating novel texture details. The experimental evaluation uses a new benchmark dataset, which is a valuable contribution, but the diversity of categories should also be considered.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Semantic Awareness:** The method successfully preserves semantic relationships during texture transfer, leading to more plausible and consistent results compared to baselines.
    *   **Generalization:** Despite training on a single example, the method generalizes well to diverse shapes within the same category.
    *   **Efficiency:** The triplane-based architecture enables fast inference times, making it suitable for large scenes.
    *   **New Dataset:** The authors created a new benchmark dataset specifically for the texture transfer task.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generative Capabilities:** The method cannot synthesize novel texture details that are not present in the source mesh.
    *   **Orientation Dependence:** The method expects the target shape to be at the same orientation as the source shape. This is now fixed since the paper now says "When enabling arbitrary rotations during training, the model becomes invariant to the object's orientation..."
    *   **Cross-Category Limitations:**  Transferring textures between objects with limited semantic overlap results in stochastic texture patterns, which limits the applicability of the approach.
    *   **Reliance on Pre-trained Features:** The method depends on the quality and accuracy of the pre-trained semantic features (Diff3F).
    *   **Lack of Details on Data** The details related to the number of objects, and where were objects acquired and how did the filtering based on BLIP work is not discussed, and should be.
    *   **Limitations in Figure 10 is also there** This is very important since that highlights what would fail if not enough details are there, and what is the main weakness of the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical and efficient solution for single-example texture transfer. Future research could build upon this work by incorporating generative capabilities to synthesize novel texture details and address the limitations related to orientation dependence and cross-category transfer. There should be better integration with foundation models with cross attention which can provide context.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of 7. The method provides valuable advancements in texture transfer. The integration of pre-trained semantic features and the triplane representation for efficient texture mapping is a novel idea. This allows the method to generalize from single meshes and keep plausible semantic properties for a decent performance. The new dataset will be very helpful for follow up work. However, the core limitations regarding generative capabilities and semantic feature reliance do curb the novelty and scope, this is what drops the scope and novelty, and limits the scalability of the method. In comparison with state-of-the-art methods, which are also very limited in capabilities, this paper is not ground breaking but is a decent advancement.

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Fed-NDIF: A Noise-Embedded Federated Diffusion Model For Low-Count Whole-Body PET Denoising
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16635v1)
- **Authors**: Yinchi Zhou, Huidong Xie, Menghua Xia, Qiong Liu, Bo Zhou, Tianqi Chen, Jun Hou, Liang Guo, Xinyuan Zheng, Hanzhong Wang, Biao Li, Axel Rominger, Kuangyu Shi, Nicha C. Dvorneka, Chi Liu
- **Abstract**: Low-count positron emission tomography (LCPET) imaging can reduce patients' exposure to radiation but often suffers from increased image noise and reduced lesion detectability, necessitating effective denoising techniques. Diffusion models have shown promise in LCPET denoising for recovering degraded image quality. However, training such models requires large and diverse datasets, which are challenging to obtain in the medical domain. To address data scarcity and privacy concerns, we combine diffusion models with federated learning -- a decentralized training approach where models are trained individually at different sites, and their parameters are aggregated on a central server over multiple iterations. The variation in scanner types and image noise levels within and across institutions poses additional challenges for federated learning in LCPET denoising. In this study, we propose a novel noise-embedded federated learning diffusion model (Fed-NDIF) to address these challenges, leveraging a multicenter dataset and varying count levels. Our approach incorporates liver normalized standard deviation (NSTD) noise embedding into a 2.5D diffusion model and utilizes the Federated Averaging (FedAvg) algorithm to aggregate locally trained models into a global model, which is subsequently fine-tuned on local datasets to optimize performance and obtain personalized models. Extensive validation on datasets from the University of Bern, Ruijin Hospital in Shanghai, and Yale-New Haven Hospital demonstrates the superior performance of our method in enhancing image quality and improving lesion quantification. The Fed-NDIF model shows significant improvements in PSNR, SSIM, and NMSE of the entire 3D volume, as well as enhanced lesion detectability and quantification, compared to local diffusion models and federated UNet-based models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Fed-NDIF: A Noise-Embedded Federated Diffusion Model For Low-Count Whole-Body PET Denoising" addresses the problem of denoising low-count PET (LCPET) images, a crucial step for reducing radiation exposure in patients. It proposes a novel federated learning approach called Fed-NDIF, which combines diffusion models with federated learning to overcome data scarcity and privacy concerns associated with medical imaging datasets. A key innovation is the incorporation of liver-normalized standard deviation (NSTD) as a noise embedding into the 2.5D diffusion model, designed to handle the variability in scanner types and noise levels across different institutions. The method uses federated averaging (FedAvg) to aggregate models trained locally at different sites and then fine-tunes the global model locally. The authors demonstrate the superiority of Fed-NDIF over local diffusion models and federated UNet-based approaches using datasets from multiple institutions (Bern, Ruijin, Yale-New Haven), showing improved image quality metrics (PSNR, SSIM, NMSE) and enhanced lesion detectability and quantification.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel combination of existing techniques (federated learning and diffusion models) with a critical adaptation for the specific challenges of LCPET denoising. The most innovative aspects include:

    *   *Noise Embedding Strategy:* The incorporation of liver NSTD is a significant contribution. The idea is that by calculating NSTD from the liver region of interest in low count PET images and using that measure as input to the model alongside the diffusion training allows the model to dynamically respond to changes in noise between different scanners, acquisition parameters, and body habitus.

    *   *Federated Learning for Diffusion Models in PET:* While federated learning has been applied to CNN-based medical image analysis, its application to diffusion models in the PET denoising context, especially with the considerations for noise heterogeneity, has less prior work.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a significant problem in PET imaging: reducing radiation exposure while maintaining image quality for accurate diagnosis. The federated learning approach mitigates data scarcity and privacy issues, which are major roadblocks in medical imaging research. The demonstrated improvements in image quality and lesion quantification have direct clinical implications, potentially leading to more accurate diagnoses with lower radiation doses.
* **Strengths:**
    * Thorough experimental validation on multi-institutional datasets, including different PET scanners and count levels.
    * Quantifiable improvements in image quality and lesion quantification over multiple baselines.
    * Clear articulation of the problem and challenges of LCPET denoising.
    * Well-structured and presented.
    * Comprehensive ablation studies demonstrating the importance of different components of the proposed framework (noise embedding, federated learning, fine-tuning).

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   *Computational Cost:* The paper mentions the concern of training time and the costs of federated diffusion models, but doesn't delve into specific details regarding training duration, hardware requirements, or scalability. An investigation regarding training time and communication efficiency will enhance the quality of the paper.

    *   *2.5D Limitation:* While the authors acknowledge the 2.5D approach as a limitation and suggest extensions to 3D, this could affect the reconstruction quality of the entire body.

    *   *Limited diversity for the Lesion Evaluation Dataset:* Table II used only Yale dataset for Lesion evaluation metrics which is a limitation.

    *   *Reliance on nn-UNet for Liver Segmentation:* The proposed method requires a pre-trained nn-UNet model for liver segmentation, which will introduce dependencies. How would the model perform if liver segmentation module failed?

*   **Potential Impact:** The Fed-NDIF framework has the potential to become a valuable tool for LCPET image denoising, enabling wider adoption of dose reduction strategies. The incorporation of NSTD embedding could serve as a template for addressing dataset and scanner heterogeneity in other medical imaging applications. The federated learning approach also demonstrates a viable pathway for collaborative research in medical imaging while preserving data privacy.
* **Overall:** The paper makes a significant contribution by presenting a novel and effective method for LCPET denoising. The NSTD embedding and thorough validation across multiple datasets and count levels enhance the novelty and significance of the work. While there are limitations regarding the computation resources and 2.5D architecture, the improvements shown justify its positive impact on the field.

### Accelerating Antibiotic Discovery with Large Language Models and Knowledge Graphs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16655v1)
- **Authors**: Maxime Delmas, Magdalena Wysocka, Danilo Gusicuma, André Freitas
- **Abstract**: The discovery of novel antibiotics is critical to address the growing antimicrobial resistance (AMR). However, pharmaceutical industries face high costs (over $1 billion), long timelines, and a high failure rate, worsened by the rediscovery of known compounds. We propose an LLM-based pipeline that acts as an alarm system, detecting prior evidence of antibiotic activity to prevent costly rediscoveries. The system integrates organism and chemical literature into a Knowledge Graph (KG), ensuring taxonomic resolution, synonym handling, and multi-level evidence classification. We tested the pipeline on a private list of 73 potential antibiotic-producing organisms, disclosing 12 negative hits for evaluation. The results highlight the effectiveness of the pipeline for evidence reviewing, reducing false negatives, and accelerating decision-making. The KG for negative hits and the user interface for interactive exploration will be made publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces an LLM-based pipeline designed to accelerate antibiotic discovery by acting as an alarm system to prevent the rediscovery of known compounds. It integrates organism and chemical literature using a Knowledge Graph (KG), tackling taxonomic resolution, synonym handling, and multi-level evidence classification. The pipeline was tested on a private list of organisms, demonstrating its effectiveness in evidence reviewing, reducing false negatives, and accelerating decision-making. The authors intend to release the KG and a user interface for interactive exploration.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Problem Focus:** The paper addresses a significant and well-defined problem in antibiotic discovery: the costly and time-consuming rediscovery of known compounds.
    *   **Integration of Tools:** The pipeline effectively integrates LLMs, Knowledge Graphs, and public data sources (PubMed, LOTUS, GBIF) to tackle the problem. The combination leverages the strengths of each approach.
    *   **Taxonomic Resolution:** The KG's emphasis on taxonomic and nomenclature resolution is critical and a clear strength, given the documented instability in these areas.
    *   **Evidence Classification:** The alert levels (Strong, Medium, Weak) provide a valuable prioritization mechanism for reviewers, particularly important with the potentially overwhelming number of alerts.
    *   **Real-World Application:** The evaluation on a private, industrial dataset lends credibility to the approach and highlights its practical relevance.
    *   **Error Analysis:** The error analysis provides useful insights into the system's limitations and directions for future improvement. The discussion of PubMed's coverage gaps is particularly relevant.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Data:** While the intention to release the KG is valuable, the reliance on a private initial list of 73 organisms limits the generalizability of the results. The evaluation on only 12 discarded hits also represents a modest sample size.
    *   **Dependence on External Resources:** The pipeline's performance hinges on the quality and coverage of public databases (PubMed, LOTUS, GBIF). Gaps in these resources, as demonstrated in the paper, directly impact the system's effectiveness. The error analysis notes failures due to the absence of abstracts or full text.
    *   **LLM Prompting Strategy:** The paper describes the prompts used for evidence extraction and classification, however, the effectiveness of the prompts compared to alternative methods could be discussed. While zero-shot is effective, it's not necessairly the 'best' possible.
    *   **Lack of Comparative Evaluation:** While the ablation study using only LOTUS is helpful, a more comprehensive comparison against other existing approaches or baseline methods would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Accessibility of Proprietary Data:** The exclusion of proprietary data sources (like CAS SciFinder) from the main pipeline demonstrates limitations and suggests a dependence upon external proprietary data is still necessary.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The approach is novel in its integration of LLMs and KGs specifically to prevent rediscovery in antibiotic research. While LLMs have been used in biomedical literature review (as the paper acknowledges), this targeted application and the emphasis on non-novelty detection is a key differentiating factor.
    *   The practical impact is significant, as it has the potential to save pharmaceutical companies substantial time and resources by identifying previously reported activities early in the drug discovery process.
    *   The creation and release of a publicly accessible KG for this purpose would be a valuable contribution to the research community.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper could influence how pharmaceutical companies approach early-stage antibiotic discovery by prompting them to adopt similar LLM-based alert systems.
    *   The KG and user interface could become a valuable resource for researchers in the field, facilitating literature review and knowledge discovery.
    *   The paper could encourage further research into the use of LLMs and KGs for other similar challenges in drug discovery and biomedical research.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid approach to a critical problem, integrating LLMs and KGs effectively. While the evaluation could be strengthened with a larger dataset and more comprehensive comparisons, the real-world application and practical focus of the work justify a good score. The potential for the KG and user interface to become valuable resources also contributes positively. Given the scope of limitations, I am providing a score of *7* for the potential of this paper. Further developments will have to make this model more robust to increase its usability.

### Code Evolution Graphs: Understanding Large Language Model Driven Design of Algorithms
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16668v1)
- **Authors**: Niki van Stein, Anna V. Kononova, Lars Kotthoff, Thomas Bäck
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated great promise in generating code, especially when used inside an evolutionary computation framework to iteratively optimize the generated algorithms. However, in some cases they fail to generate competitive algorithms or the code optimization stalls, and we are left with no recourse because of a lack of understanding of the generation process and generated codes. We present a novel approach to mitigate this problem by enabling users to analyze the generated codes inside the evolutionary process and how they evolve over repeated prompting of the LLM. We show results for three benchmark problem classes and demonstrate novel insights. In particular, LLMs tend to generate more complex code with repeated prompting, but additional complexity can hurt algorithmic performance in some cases. Different LLMs have different coding ``styles'' and generated code tends to be dissimilar to other LLMs. These two findings suggest that using different LLMs inside the code evolution frameworks might produce higher performing code than using only one LLM.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Code Evolution Graphs (CEGs), a novel framework for analyzing the evolution of algorithms generated and optimized using Large Language Models (LLMs) within evolutionary computation. CEGs combine static code analysis (using Abstract Syntax Trees or ASTs), graph-based representations, and complexity metrics to understand the dynamics of algorithm generation. The authors apply CEGs to algorithms generated by LLaMEA (a Large Language Model Evolutionary Algorithm), LLaMEA-HPO (with hyperparameter optimization), and Evolution of Heuristics (EoH) on three benchmark tasks: Black-Box Optimization (BBO), Online Bin Packing (OBP), and the Traveling Salesperson Problem (TSP). The analysis focuses on structural modularity, logical complexity, and scalability of the generated code. The paper demonstrates that LLMs tend to generate more complex code with repeated prompting, but this complexity isn't always beneficial. It also shows that different LLMs exhibit distinct coding styles, which suggests using a diverse set of LLMs could yield superior results.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the introduction of CEGs as a tool for *visualizing and analyzing* the evolutionary process of LLM-generated code. While individual components like AST analysis, graph metrics, and complexity measures are established techniques, their integration *specifically for understanding LLM-driven algorithm design* is novel.  The application to different LLM frameworks (LLaMEA, EoH) and problem domains (BBO, OBP, TSP) is also a strength.

**Significance:**  The significance of this work stems from addressing a critical gap in the understanding of LLM-driven algorithm design.  These systems often behave like black boxes; it's difficult to understand *why* a particular algorithm is generated and *how* it's evolving. The CEG framework provides a mechanism to open this box, offering insights into the impact of various factors (LLM used, task, evolutionary strategy) on the resulting code and its performance. This opens up possibilities for better controlling and guiding the algorithm generation process. However, it would have been more impactful if the findings had directly led to concrete proposals to *improve* existing LLM-based algorithm design systems, rather than primarily focusing on *understanding*.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Methodology:**  The paper clearly explains the CEG framework and the methods used for feature extraction and analysis.
*   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The study systematically analyzes various structural and complexity features of the generated code.
*   **Empirical Evaluation:**  The application of CEGs to multiple benchmark tasks and LLM frameworks provides a strong empirical foundation.
*   **Actionable Insights:** The findings about LLM-specific coding styles and the impact of code complexity offer practical guidance for future research in the field.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Direct Impact on Algorithm Performance:** While the CEG framework provides valuable insights, it doesn't directly translate into a concrete improvement in the *performance* of LLM-generated algorithms. The focus is more on understanding than on optimization.
*   **Feature Selection Justification:** While a comprehensive set of AST-based features is extracted, the justification for their selection could be stronger.  Why were these particular features chosen over others? How do they specifically relate to the algorithm design process?
*   **Correlation vs. Causation:** The correlation analysis highlights relationships between code features and fitness, but it doesn't establish causality.  It's difficult to determine whether a particular feature is *driving* the performance or simply *correlated* with it.  A more in-depth causal analysis, perhaps using interventional techniques, would strengthen the conclusions.
*   **Lack of a Control Experiment:** A control experiment where the code complexity is actively managed (e.g., penalized or rewarded) within the evolutionary process would provide much stronger evidence to support the claim that LLMs often generate too complex code. Without this type of experiment, it is difficult to say with certainty that the complexity actually hurts algorithmic performance in cases where it shows negative correlation.

**Potential Influence:**

The CEG framework could become a valuable tool for researchers working on LLM-driven algorithm design. It can facilitate a more systematic and data-driven approach to understanding the strengths and weaknesses of different LLM-based systems, leading to more targeted improvements in the future. Other researchers could use the methods outlined in this paper and apply them to other existing LLM-based code design methods. However, its long-term influence will depend on whether it can be used to *directly improve* the performance and robustness of LLM-generated algorithms.

**Score:** 7/10

**Rationale for the Score:** The paper presents a novel and well-executed methodology (CEGs) that provides valuable insights into the evolution of LLM-generated code. The empirical analysis is comprehensive, and the findings are actionable. However, the lack of direct impact on algorithm performance, the need for a stronger justification for feature selection, and limited causal analysis keep it from scoring higher.

- **Classification**: cs.NE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Echoes of Power: Investigating Geopolitical Bias in US and China Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16679v1)
- **Authors**: Andre G. C. Pacheco, Athus Cavalini, Giovanni Comarela
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have emerged as powerful tools for generating human-like text, transforming human-machine interactions. However, their widespread adoption has raised concerns about their potential to influence public opinion and shape political narratives. In this work, we investigate the geopolitical biases in US and Chinese LLMs, focusing on how these models respond to questions related to geopolitics and international relations. We collected responses from ChatGPT and DeepSeek to a set of geopolitical questions and evaluated their outputs through both qualitative and quantitative analyses. Our findings show notable biases in both models, reflecting distinct ideological perspectives and cultural influences. However, despite these biases, for a set of questions, the models' responses are more aligned than expected, indicating that they can address sensitive topics without necessarily presenting directly opposing viewpoints. This study highlights the potential of LLMs to shape public discourse and underscores the importance of critically assessing AI-generated content, particularly in politically sensitive contexts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Echoes of Power: Investigating Geopolitical Bias in US and China Large Language Models" investigates geopolitical biases in Large Language Models (LLMs) developed in the US (ChatGPT) and China (DeepSeek). The authors formulated a benchmark of 50 questions on geopolitics and international relations and analyzed the models' responses both quantitatively (similarity and disagreement metrics based on sentence embeddings and LLM-based disagreement scores) and qualitatively (thematic analysis of the content, tone, and framing). The study reveals that both models exhibit biases reflecting the geopolitical interests of their respective countries. DeepSeek employs "hard locks" to avoid sensitive topics within the Chinese context, while ChatGPT does not have such explicit restrictions. While the models sometimes show alignment in responses, framing differences and soft biases are present. The authors conclude by discussing the implications of these biases for public opinion and political discourse, emphasizing the need for critical engagement with AI-generated content.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Timely and Relevant Topic:** The research addresses a crucial and increasingly important issue: bias in LLMs, particularly concerning geopolitics and national narratives. This is a significant concern as LLMs are increasingly used for information retrieval and content generation.
    *   **Comparative Analysis:** The comparison between a US-developed and a Chinese-developed LLM provides valuable insights into how different geopolitical contexts influence model behavior.
    *   **Mixed-Methods Approach:** The combination of quantitative (similarity, disagreement) and qualitative (thematic analysis) methods offers a more comprehensive understanding of the nuances of bias in the models' responses.
    *   **Dataset Availability:**  Making the dataset public promotes reproducibility and allows for further research on this topic.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Models:** The study focuses on only two LLMs, while the AI landscape is rapidly evolving.  Generalizing the findings to all US and Chinese LLMs might be problematic.
    *   **Single Snapshot in Time:** The analysis is based on responses collected at a specific point in time. LLMs are constantly being updated, so biases and responses can change over time. A longitudinal study would provide a more robust analysis.
    *   **Potential Prompting Bias:** While the authors avoid sophisticated prompting techniques, the choice of questions themselves could introduce biases. The questions could be interpreted or framed differently by the LLMs.
    *   **Subjectivity in Qualitative Analysis:** The thematic analysis, while insightful, relies on human interpretation and is thus subject to the authors' own biases. The divergence metric is also determined by other LLMs, which can have biases of their own.
    *   **Web interface Level:** One of the key findings regarding hard locks in DeepSeek has been identified to operate at the Web Interface level which limits the significance of this analysis.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   While the topic of bias in LLMs is not entirely new, the specific focus on geopolitical bias and the direct comparison of US and Chinese models represent a worthwhile contribution. The findings highlighting the "hard locks" in DeepSeek and the subtler framing differences in both models are significant.
    *   The paper's significance lies in raising awareness about the potential for LLMs to shape public opinion and reinforce national narratives in subtle ways. The findings underscore the need for greater transparency and accountability in the development and deployment of AI technologies.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper could influence future research by providing a framework for analyzing geopolitical biases in LLMs. The dataset can serve as a valuable resource for other researchers.
    *   The findings may encourage LLM developers to be more mindful of the potential for bias and to implement strategies to mitigate it.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper makes a timely contribution to a relevant field, several limitations, including the limited model selection and snapshot approach, prevent it from receiving a high score.  The reliance on human interpretation in the qualitative analysis also introduces a degree of subjectivity. However, the comparative aspect and the identification of "hard locks" add value. Furthermore, the study can be easily replicated by other researchers, which enhances the validity of this work.

- **Score**: 6/10

### WaveFM: A High-Fidelity and Efficient Vocoder Based on Flow Matching
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16689v1)
- **Authors**: Tianze Luo, Xingchen Miao, Wenbo Duan
- **Abstract**: Flow matching offers a robust and stable approach to training diffusion models. However, directly applying flow matching to neural vocoders can result in subpar audio quality. In this work, we present WaveFM, a reparameterized flow matching model for mel-spectrogram conditioned speech synthesis, designed to enhance both sample quality and generation speed for diffusion vocoders. Since mel-spectrograms represent the energy distribution of waveforms, WaveFM adopts a mel-conditioned prior distribution instead of a standard Gaussian prior to minimize unnecessary transportation costs during synthesis. Moreover, while most diffusion vocoders rely on a single loss function, we argue that incorporating auxiliary losses, including a refined multi-resolution STFT loss, can further improve audio quality. To speed up inference without degrading sample quality significantly, we introduce a tailored consistency distillation method for WaveFM. Experiment results demonstrate that our model achieves superior performance in both quality and efficiency compared to previous diffusion vocoders, while enabling waveform generation in a single inference step.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the WaveFM paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces WaveFM, a novel neural vocoder based on flow matching for mel-spectrogram conditioned speech synthesis. It addresses the limitations of directly applying flow matching to neural vocoders, which often results in subpar audio quality and slow generation speeds. WaveFM incorporates several key innovations: (1) a mel-conditioned prior distribution to minimize unnecessary transportation costs during synthesis, (2) auxiliary losses (including a refined multi-resolution STFT loss) to improve audio quality, and (3) a tailored consistency distillation method to accelerate inference without sacrificing audio quality. Experimental results demonstrate that WaveFM achieves superior performance in both quality and efficiency compared to previous diffusion vocoders, allowing for single-step waveform generation. The paper also highlights the model's strong generalization capabilities on out-of-distribution musical data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a number of novel contributions. The reparameterized flow matching objective allowing for auxiliary losses is significant. The use of a mel-conditioned prior, while not entirely new in diffusion models, is adapted to the flow matching framework in a non-trivial way that addresses issues of distribution mismatch. The tailored consistency distillation method to fit the reparameterized objective demonstrates a clear engineering contribution that results in impressive speed improvements. The refined multi-resolution STFT loss including phase angle losses and gradient/Laplacian operators enhances perceptual quality.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical issue in neural vocoders: the trade-off between audio quality and generation speed. By achieving both high fidelity and efficient inference, WaveFM represents a significant step forward, making high-quality speech synthesis more practical for real-time applications. The strong performance on out-of-distribution musical data indicates good generalization ability.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-designed architecture with justified design choices.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation including both subjective and objective metrics.
    *   Strong results demonstrating significant improvements over existing methods in terms of both quality and speed.
    *   Detailed ablation studies to validate the effectiveness of individual components.
    *   Addresses a current important issue, namely the limitations of prior work in generative modeling for audio.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The performance gap between multi-step generation and the distilled single-step generation still remains significant, indicating room for improvement in the distillation technique.
    *   Ethical Considerations: The paper acknowledges the potential for misuse of high-fidelity speech synthesis technology, which is good, but doesn't go into depth about potential mitigation strategies.
    *   Lack of details on the exact network architecture of the auxiliary loss functions (STFT and mel-spectrogram losses).

*   **Potential Influence:** WaveFM has the potential to influence the development of future neural vocoders by popularizing the use of flow matching with the presented enhancements, specifically for speech synthesis. The techniques demonstrated in the paper, particularly the mel-conditioned prior and tailored distillation method, could be adopted and adapted by other researchers working on similar problems. The out-of-distribution generalization results also point towards potentially wider applications beyond speech.
    *   While the contributions are solid and well-executed, there is a reliance on components and techniques derived from other domains, reducing the relative novelty compared to if they had constructed it all from scratch. The performance gains are clearly present however, making the design choices and innovations worthwhile.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7.5** to this paper. The paper presents a solid and well-engineered solution to a relevant problem in the field of neural vocoders. The novelty of the proposed architecture and training techniques, along with the substantial improvements in both audio quality and generation speed warrant this score. The clear and concise writing and comprehensive experimental validation further contribute to the paper's value. However, it is worth noting that it is not pushing the boundaries of the field significantly due to the incremental adaptation of techniques, and the discussion of ethical considerations could be expanded. Thus, the score falls between a good (7) and very good (8) categorization.

Score: 7.5

### ATOM: A Framework of Detecting Query-Based Model Extraction Attacks for Graph Neural Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16693v1)
- **Authors**: Zhan Cheng, Bolin Shen, Tianming Sha, Yuan Gao, Shibo Li, Yushun Dong
- **Abstract**: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have gained traction in Graph-based Machine Learning as a Service (GMLaaS) platforms, yet they remain vulnerable to graph-based model extraction attacks (MEAs), where adversaries reconstruct surrogate models by querying the victim model. Existing defense mechanisms, such as watermarking and fingerprinting, suffer from poor real-time performance, susceptibility to evasion, or reliance on post-attack verification, making them inadequate for handling the dynamic characteristics of graph-based MEA variants. To address these limitations, we propose ATOM, a novel real-time MEA detection framework tailored for GNNs. ATOM integrates sequential modeling and reinforcement learning to dynamically detect evolving attack patterns, while leveraging $k$-core embedding to capture the structural properties, enhancing detection precision. Furthermore, we provide theoretical analysis to characterize query behaviors and optimize detection strategies. Extensive experiments on multiple real-world datasets demonstrate that ATOM outperforms existing approaches in detection performance, maintaining stable across different time steps, thereby offering a more effective defense mechanism for GMLaaS environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ATOM, a novel framework for real-time detection of model extraction attacks (MEAs) targeting Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) deployed in Graph-based Machine Learning as a Service (GMLaaS) environments. ATOM addresses limitations of existing defense mechanisms (watermarking, fingerprinting) by integrating sequential modeling and reinforcement learning to dynamically detect evolving attack patterns.  It uses k-core embedding to capture structural properties for enhanced detection precision and provides theoretical analysis characterizing query behaviors and optimizing detection strategies.  The authors claim that ATOM outperforms existing approaches in detection performance while maintaining stability across time steps, offering a more effective defense for GMLaaS.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the application of sequential modeling and reinforcement learning specifically to the problem of MEA detection on GNNs in GMLaaS. While the individual components (GRU, PPO, k-core centrality) are not novel in themselves, their integrated application in this context appears to be a unique contribution. The claim of it being the "first framework" for proactive graph-based MEA detection in GMLaaS if substantiated, further reinforces this point.

*   **Significance:** The increasing adoption of GMLaaS makes it crucial to address security vulnerabilities like MEAs.  A proactive detection mechanism, like ATOM, could significantly enhance the security of these platforms, protecting both service providers and users. The paper's focus on GNNs is particularly relevant given their rising popularity and sensitivity of the data they often handle (e.g., financial fraud, biomolecular interactions).

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** ATOM addresses multiple challenges associated with GNN MEA detection, including the sequential nature of queries, dynamic attack strategies, and the need to consider structural information.
    *   **Theoretical Foundation:** The theoretical analysis, linking query interaction to dominating set problems and deriving bounds on query behavior, provides a solid mathematical basis for the framework.
    *   **Strong Empirical Evaluation:** The experiments, using multiple real-world datasets and comparing ATOM against various baselines, offer evidence of its effectiveness. The ablation study and parameter sensitivity analysis provide further insights into ATOM's design choices.
    * **Addressing GMLaaS limitations**: The active attacker simulation by exploiting the one-hop subgraph constraint enforced by GMLaaS is more realistic as the model providers typically do not allow direct access to the whole graph structure.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simulation-Based Attack Generation:** The reliance on active learning-based attack *simulation* is a potential weakness. The effectiveness of ATOM depends on how well these simulated attacks reflect real-world attacker behavior. If attackers employ strategies not considered in the simulation, ATOM's performance could degrade.  The paper would benefit from a more detailed discussion of the limitations of the simulation and how it addresses potential gaps in coverage.
    * **Limited Real-World Datasets**: While multiple datasets are used, most belong to specific areas, for example citation networks. Although the authors emphasize the generalisation ability of ATOM, the robustness across diverse GMLaaS real-world tasks requires a further validation.
    *   **Lack of Comparison to More Recent DNN defenses:** While the paper compares against classical defenses like watermarking and fingerprinting, it doesn't fully explore and benchmark against recent, highly effective DNN attack detection techniques that might be adaptable to the graph domain.
    *   **Assumptions:** The assumptions about the attacker's knowledge and capabilities (e.g., one-hop subgraph access) need careful consideration. While justifiable, these assumptions should be explicitly stated as limitations.

*   **Potential Impact:** If ATOM proves robust against a wider range of real-world attacks, it could become a valuable tool for securing GMLaaS platforms.  The framework's design and theoretical analysis could also inspire further research in this area.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper has merit. The use of reinforcement learning to dynamically adapt to MEAs shows a degree of originality, and it targets an increasingly significant research area, GMLaaS Security. There is solid, but potentially limited (by simulation), empirical validation.

The weaknesses around simulation-based evaluation and lack of broader benchmarks prevent it from achieving a truly high score. If the generated attack sequences well represented the attacker, this would improve the novelty and impact of this work.

### EDiT: Efficient Diffusion Transformers with Linear Compressed Attention
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16726v1)
- **Authors**: Philipp Becker, Abhinav Mehrotra, Ruchika Chavhan, Malcolm Chadwick, Luca Morreale, Mehdi Noroozi, Alberto Gil Ramos, Sourav Bhattacharya
- **Abstract**: Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) have emerged as a leading architecture for text-to-image synthesis, producing high-quality and photorealistic images. However, the quadratic scaling properties of the attention in DiTs hinder image generation with higher resolution or on devices with limited resources. This work introduces an efficient diffusion transformer (EDiT) to alleviate these efficiency bottlenecks in conventional DiTs and Multimodal DiTs (MM-DiTs). First, we present a novel linear compressed attention method that uses a multi-layer convolutional network to modulate queries with local information while keys and values are spatially aggregated. Second, we formulate a hybrid attention scheme for multi-modal inputs that combines linear attention for image-to-image interactions and standard scaled dot-product attention for interactions involving prompts. Merging these two approaches leads to an expressive, linear-time Multimodal Efficient Diffusion Transformer (MM-EDiT). We demonstrate the effectiveness of the EDiT and MM-EDiT architectures by integrating them into PixArt-Sigma(conventional DiT) and Stable Diffusion 3.5-Medium (MM-DiT), achieving up to 2.2x speedup with comparable image quality after distillation.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EDiT (Efficient Diffusion Transformer) and MM-EDiT (Multimodal Efficient Diffusion Transformer), architectures designed to improve the computational efficiency of Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) for image synthesis. The core innovation is a novel linear compressed attention mechanism. EDiT employs a multi-layer convolutional network (ConvFusion) to integrate local information into query tokens, and spatial aggregation (Spatial Compressor) of key and value tokens via depthwise convolutions. MM-EDiT extends this by using a hybrid attention scheme: linear compressed attention (EDiT) for image-to-image interactions and standard scaled dot-product attention for interactions involving text prompts.  The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of these architectures by distilling PixArt-Σ (a conventional DiT) and Stable Diffusion 3.5-Medium (an MM-DiT), achieving speedups (up to 2.2x) while maintaining comparable image quality.  They also perform ablations to demonstrate the efficacy of the individual components of their proposed architectures. Finally, the paper shows that the models run faster on mobile hardware.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel combination of techniques. The ConvFusion and Spatial Compressor modules are unique, although they build upon the existing concepts of linear attention and token compression.  The hybrid attention scheme for multimodal inputs is also a good idea.  The overall architecture, while not revolutionary, appears to represent a practical and useful engineering contribution. However, there's considerable prior work in both linearizing attention and token compression, so the novelty is incremental rather than groundbreaking.

* **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in its potential to make DiTs more accessible and practical, especially for high-resolution image generation and deployment on resource-constrained devices (mobile phones). By reducing the computational cost of attention, the authors enable faster inference without sacrificing image quality.  This is valuable given the growing demand for efficient and scalable AI models. Also, the ablation studies validate the design choices of combining ConvFusion and Spatial Compression. The improvements on mobile hardware are also significant.

* **Strengths:**
    * Clear and well-written.
    * The proposed architectures are well-motivated.
    * Thorough experimental evaluation, including comparisons to existing methods and ablation studies.
    * Demonstrates real-world impact by evaluating performance on mobile hardware.
    * Successfully integrates with two separate architectures, a standard diffusion transformer and a multimodal one.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The techniques used are mostly incremental improvements on existing ideas. There is no clear mathematical framework or deep theoretical contribution.
    * The evaluation is primarily limited to PixArt-Σ and Stable Diffusion 3.5-Medium. Evaluating on a wider range of models (e.g., the recent Flux) would strengthen the claims of generalizability.
    * The improvements on mobile hardware show that dot-product attention with image queries is computationally expensive, but no solution is provided.
    * The method for linear attention could be improved. As the paper mentions, linear attention sacrifices image quality compared to dot-product attention.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on efficient DiTs.  The proposed techniques could be adopted by other researchers and developers to improve the performance of their own models. The hybrid attention scheme could also inspire new approaches to handling multimodal inputs in DiTs. However, the impact might be limited by the incremental nature of the contributions.
* **Justification for the score:**
    The paper provides a solid and well-engineered contribution to the field of diffusion models, with practical benefits for deployment and efficiency.  However, the reliance on existing ideas and the limited scope of the evaluation prevent it from being considered a truly exceptional contribution. The novelty is a combination of existing techniques. The impact will depend on how widely the proposed techniques are adopted by the community.

### Natural Language Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16728v1)
- **Authors**: Emiel van Miltenburg, Chenghua Lin
- **Abstract**: This article provides a brief overview of the field of Natural Language Generation. The term Natural Language Generation (NLG), in its broadest definition, refers to the study of systems that verbalize some form of information through natural language. That information could be stored in a large database or knowledge graph (in data-to-text applications), but NLG researchers may also study summarisation (text-to-text) or image captioning (image-to-text), for example. As a subfield of Natural Language Processing, NLG is closely related to other sub-disciplines such as Machine Translation (MT) and Dialog Systems. Some NLG researchers exclude MT from their definition of the field, since there is no content selection involved where the system has to determine what to say. Conversely, dialog systems do not typically fall under the header of Natural Language Generation since NLG is just one component of dialog systems (the others being Natural Language Understanding and Dialog Management). However, with the rise of Large Language Models (LLMs), different subfields of Natural Language Processing have converged on similar methodologies for the production of natural language and the evaluation of automatically generated text.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, presented as a draft for discussion and refinement:

**Summary:**

The paper is a survey of Natural Language Generation (NLG), covering its definition, relation to linguistics, applications (business, journalism, and medicine), different approaches (classical pipeline, end-to-end generation, and the use of Large Language Models), evaluation methods (human and automatic), and key challenges and ethical concerns. It highlights the evolution of NLG techniques, from rule-based systems to data-driven approaches, emphasizing the increasing importance of Large Language Models (LLMs).  The survey also acknowledges the complexities of evaluation, the tension between factuality and fluency, and the growing relevance of ethical considerations due to the increasing capabilities and widespread use of NLG technologies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty is limited as it primarily synthesizes existing knowledge in the field.  Surveys of NLG exist, and many cover similar ground.  The specific inclusion of LLMs is a timely update, but the discussion of LLMs in NLG, applications, and challenges are well-documented within the last few years of research. The dual use implications are valuable, but this is also an expanding area of discussion.

**Significance:** The significance of this survey lies in providing a comprehensive overview for newcomers to the field and a consolidated update on recent developments. It serves as a valuable resource for researchers seeking a high-level understanding of NLG, especially with the rapid advancements in LLMs.  However, the paper lacks deep insights or novel perspectives that would significantly advance the field. The inclusion of LLMs is a strength, but it doesn't offer a fundamentally new framework or challenge existing assumptions.

**Strengths:**

*   **Broad Coverage:**  The paper offers a good overview of the NLG landscape, encompassing historical context, various applications, and different approaches.
*   **Clear Structure:** The organization is logical, allowing readers to easily grasp the key concepts and follow the evolution of NLG.
*   **Updated Information:** The discussion of LLMs and their impact on NLG is timely and relevant, reflecting the current state of the field.
*   **Balanced Perspective:** It acknowledges the limitations and challenges associated with different NLG techniques, including issues like factuality, evaluation, and ethical considerations.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Depth:**  While broad, the survey lacks in-depth analysis of specific techniques or challenges.  The discussions of LLMs and evaluation methods, while relevant, could benefit from more critical scrutiny and specific examples.
*   **Lack of Original Insights:** The paper primarily summarizes existing work without offering novel perspectives or critical analyses that would significantly advance the field.  It doesn't identify previously overlooked connections or propose new research directions.
*   **Uneven Treatment of Topics:** Some areas (e.g., classical pipeline) receive more detailed explanation than others (e.g., long text generation or ethics).
*   **Limited Discussion of Negative Results:** The paper acknowledges the potential dangers of NLG misuse and the difficulty in guaranteeing that NLG output is both fluent and 100% factual.  These are important issues but require more detailed discussions.
