**Potential Influence:**  The paper could be a valuable resource for introductory courses or researchers entering the field, providing a solid foundation for further exploration.  However, its lack of groundbreaking insights limits its potential to shape future research directions.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper serves as a useful compilation of existing knowledge, providing a snapshot of NLG's current state. Its broad coverage and updated information are valuable. However, its lack of depth, original insights, and uneven treatment of topics prevent it from being a truly impactful contribution. It does not significantly advance the field beyond consolidating existing literature and therefore does not merit a high score.

**Score: 5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 5/10

### Towards Agentic Recommender Systems in the Era of Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16734v1)
- **Authors**: Chengkai Huang, Junda Wu, Yu Xia, Zixu Yu, Ruhan Wang, Tong Yu, Ruiyi Zhang, Ryan A. Rossi, Branislav Kveton, Dongruo Zhou, Julian McAuley, Lina Yao
- **Abstract**: Recent breakthroughs in Large Language Models (LLMs) have led to the emergence of agentic AI systems that extend beyond the capabilities of standalone models. By empowering LLMs to perceive external environments, integrate multimodal information, and interact with various tools, these agentic systems exhibit greater autonomy and adaptability across complex tasks. This evolution brings new opportunities to recommender systems (RS): LLM-based Agentic RS (LLM-ARS) can offer more interactive, context-aware, and proactive recommendations, potentially reshaping the user experience and broadening the application scope of RS. Despite promising early results, fundamental challenges remain, including how to effectively incorporate external knowledge, balance autonomy with controllability, and evaluate performance in dynamic, multimodal settings. In this perspective paper, we first present a systematic analysis of LLM-ARS: (1) clarifying core concepts and architectures; (2) highlighting how agentic capabilities -- such as planning, memory, and multimodal reasoning -- can enhance recommendation quality; and (3) outlining key research questions in areas such as safety, efficiency, and lifelong personalization. We also discuss open problems and future directions, arguing that LLM-ARS will drive the next wave of RS innovation. Ultimately, we foresee a paradigm shift toward intelligent, autonomous, and collaborative recommendation experiences that more closely align with users' evolving needs and complex decision-making processes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a perspective on the emergence of Agentic Recommender Systems (ARS) powered by Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). It argues that recent advancements in MLLMs, particularly their agentic capabilities like planning, memory, and tool utilization, offer opportunities to move beyond traditional, reactive recommender systems. The paper systematically analyzes LLM-ARS, clarifying core concepts, highlighting how agentic capabilities enhance recommendation quality, and outlining key research questions related to safety, efficiency, and lifelong personalization. It proposes a four-level evolution of recommender systems, culminating in agentic RSs. It also offers a formal task formulation for LLM-ARS and discusses open problems and future directions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its articulation of a vision for LLM-ARS as a distinct paradigm shift in recommender systems. While many papers apply LLMs to RS, this paper explicitly focuses on the "agentic" aspect and its implications for the future of the field. It synthesizes existing work and frameworks on both LLM agents and recommender systems to create a cohesive perspective. However, the individual components (LLMs, recommendation algorithms, agent architectures) are not novel in themselves. The originality lies in the synthesis and framing of the issue, and the problem decomposition.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to influence the direction of research in recommender systems. By explicitly highlighting the agentic nature of LLMs and their potential to create more autonomous, adaptive, and proactive recommendation experiences, the paper encourages researchers to explore new avenues. The identified research questions (RQ1-RQ7) are valuable for structuring future research efforts. The detailed analysis of user profiling, planning, memory, and action modules provides a useful framework for designing LLM-ARS systems. While the paper is largely conceptual, it is significant as it provides a timely overview of a rapidly developing field.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Articulation of Vision:** The paper provides a well-defined vision for LLM-ARS and its potential benefits.
    *   **Systematic Analysis:** The systematic analysis of LLM-ARS, including its core concepts, architectures, and research questions, is comprehensive.
    *   **Well-Structured Framework:** The paper offers a well-structured framework for designing and evaluating LLM-ARS systems.
    *   **Timeliness:** The paper addresses a timely and rapidly evolving area of research.
    *   **Comprehensive reference list:** The paper makes a solid attempt to include every possible reference, which is a valuable resource.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Lack of Empirical Validation:** The paper lacks empirical validation of the proposed concepts and frameworks. It primarily relies on synthesizing existing work.
    *   **High-Level Discussion:** The discussion remains at a relatively high level and could benefit from more concrete examples and practical insights.
    *   **Limited Technical Depth:** Although the paper presents a formal formulation, it can be enhanced by incorporating more precise implementation or design details.
    *   **Over-reliance on existing literature:** The paper is mainly a review of existing literature, and hence its value is tied to its ability to summarize and point out directions.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of research in recommender systems by encouraging a shift towards more agentic, autonomous, and interactive recommendation experiences. It could also serve as a useful reference for researchers and practitioners working in this area. The level-wise evolution of RS is valuable for understanding where the field is heading, and a comprehensive view of potential future research.

*   **Rigour:** The argument is very sound but the overall value is tied to the prior work. This framework is important in order to understand the next stage of RS research.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**
The paper is well-written and presents a compelling vision for the future of recommender systems. Its novelty lies in its framing of LLM-ARS as a distinct paradigm shift, and its significance lies in its potential to influence the direction of research. However, the lack of empirical validation and the relatively high-level discussion limit its immediate impact. Its reliance upon the LLM research that has come before it does mean that it is a timely contribution, which makes the paper helpful. Overall, the paper provides a good foundation for further research and development in this area.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Automated Harmfulness Testing for Code Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16740v1)
- **Authors**: Honghao Tan, Haibo Wang, Diany Pressato, Yisen Xu, Shin Hwei Tan
- **Abstract**: Generative AI systems powered by Large Language Models (LLMs) usually use content moderation to prevent harmful content spread. To evaluate the robustness of content moderation, several metamorphic testing techniques have been proposed to test content moderation software. However, these techniques mainly focus on general users (e.g., text and image generation). Meanwhile, a recent study shows that developers consider using harmful keywords when naming software artifacts to be an unethical behavior. Exposure to harmful content in software artifacts can negatively impact the mental health of developers, making content moderation for Code Large Language Models (Code LLMs) essential. We conduct a preliminary study on program transformations that can be misused to introduce harmful content into auto-generated code, identifying 32 such transformations. To address this, we propose CHT, a coverage-guided harmfulness testing framework that generates prompts using diverse transformations and harmful keywords injected into benign programs. CHT evaluates output damage to assess potential risks in LLM-generated explanations and code. Our evaluation of four Code LLMs and GPT-4o-mini reveals that content moderation in LLM-based code generation is easily bypassed. To enhance moderation, we propose a two-phase approach that first detects harmful content before generating output, improving moderation effectiveness by 483.76\%.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Automated Harmfulness Testing for Code Large Language Models" addresses the important problem of harmful content generation by Code Large Language Models (Code LLMs). The authors propose a novel framework, CHT (Coverage-guided Harmfulness Testing), which systematically generates prompts with harmful keywords injected into benign code, and then analyzes the LLM's output (modified code and natural language explanation) to assess the potential harm.  The framework leverages a coverage-guided approach to ensure diverse testing across different harm categories. The paper also explores techniques to improve content moderation in Code LLMs, specifically using function calling. Empirical evaluations on multiple Code LLMs (including DeepSeek-coder, CodeLlama, CodeGemma, and Qwen2.5-coder, plus a general LLM GPT-4o-mini) demonstrate the effectiveness of CHT in identifying vulnerabilities related to harmful content generation in these models. The results show that current content moderation in LLMs is easily bypassed and that incorporating a preliminary harmful content check before code generation significantly improves robustness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several key areas:
    *   **Targeting Code LLMs:**  Existing work on harmful content detection primarily focuses on general language models or image generation, while this paper specifically targets Code LLMs. This is a significant contribution given the increasing use of these models in software development and the potential risks associated with harmful code.
    *   **Coverage-Guided Approach:** The use of a coverage-guided approach to ensure diverse testing across harm categories is a novel and valuable aspect of the work.  This systematic approach helps address the limitations of random testing or using existing datasets that may not adequately cover all harm categories.
    *   **Output Damage Measurement:** The paper introduces a fine-grained approach to evaluate the output of LLMs by classifying and scoring the degree of harm based on whether or not the code generation was successful and/or if a warning was provided. This goes beyond simply checking if content moderation was bypassed.
    *   **Improving Content Moderation:**  The paper proposes and evaluates a concrete method to enhance content moderation of Code LLMs using function calling. This shows not only the identification of problem, but potential solution.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical issue with important implications for software development and responsible AI:
    *   **Real-World Relevance:**  Harmful content in code can have detrimental effects on developers and the software they create.  The paper's focus on this specific problem makes it highly relevant and timely.
    *   **Practical Impact:**  The CHT framework provides a practical tool for evaluating and improving the safety of Code LLMs.  The results of the empirical evaluations provide valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of existing models and can inform the development of safer Code LLMs.
    *   **Ethical Implications:**  By addressing the issue of harmful content generation, the paper contributes to the broader goal of responsible AI and the development of ethical and safe AI systems.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Clearly Defined Problem:** The paper clearly defines the problem of harmful content generation by Code LLMs and provides a compelling rationale for addressing it.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** The CHT framework is well-defined and implemented, with a clear description of its components and their interactions.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The empirical evaluations are comprehensive, including multiple Code LLMs and a general LLM, as well as a detailed analysis of the results.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors made the datasets and codes publicly available, which helps to make the result more reproducible.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Language Support:** The framework currently supports only Java programs and Java naming conventions. Expanding the framework to support other programming languages is an important area for future work.
    *   **Complexity of Harm:** While the paper considers a relatively broad range of harm categories, the concept of "harm" is itself complex and multi-faceted. Future work could explore more nuanced definitions of harm and incorporate more sophisticated techniques for assessing the potential impact of harmful content. The analysis focuses primarily on keyword detection in generated code. Further investigation into more subtle forms of harm (e.g., logic bombs disguised as harmless code) would be valuable.
    *   **Evaluation Bias**: The 20/80 sampling methodology should have a better justification, the result that extracted keyword for warning message in LLMs relies on this sampling process, which might introduce biases.

* **Potential Influence:**
    *   **Raises Awareness:** The paper raises awareness of the issue of harmful content generation by Code LLMs, which is likely to become increasingly important as these models become more widely used.
    *   **Informs Development:** The results of the empirical evaluations can inform the development of safer Code LLMs by highlighting the strengths and weaknesses of existing models and by providing guidance on how to improve content moderation.
    *   **Inspires Future Research:** The paper lays the foundation for future research on this topic and inspires the development of more sophisticated techniques for detecting and mitigating harmful content in code.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a strong contribution by addressing a relevant and growing problem with Code LLMs. The novelty of the approach is solid, especially with the coverage-guided methodology and the evaluation of output damage. The empirical evaluations are thorough and informative, and the authors provide practical suggestions for improving the robustness of Code LLMs. While the framework currently supports only Java and could benefit from a more complex analysis of harm, the overall impact of the paper is significant and deserves a score of 8. It's a strong, well-executed piece of work that provides valuable insights and will likely inspire further research in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### Current and Future Use of Large Language Models for Knowledge Work
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16774v1)
- **Authors**: Michelle Brachman, Amina El-Ashry, Casey Dugan, Werner Geyer
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have introduced a paradigm shift in interaction with AI technology, enabling knowledge workers to complete tasks by specifying their desired outcome in natural language. LLMs have the potential to increase productivity and reduce tedious tasks in an unprecedented way. A systematic study of LLM adoption for work can provide insight into how LLMs can best support these workers. To explore knowledge workers' current and desired usage of LLMs, we ran a survey (n=216). Workers described tasks they already used LLMs for, like generating code or improving text, but imagined a future with LLMs integrated into their workflows and data. We ran a second survey (n=107) a year later that validated our initial findings and provides insight into up-to-date LLM use by knowledge workers. We discuss implications for adoption and design of generative AI technologies for knowledge work.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

This paper presents a two-part study examining the current and future use of Large Language Models (LLMs) by knowledge workers. The authors conducted surveys in a large international technology company to understand how employees are using LLMs in their work and personal lives, as well as how they envision using LLMs in the future. The first survey used open-ended questions to identify common usage patterns, while the second survey validated those patterns and measured their frequency. The findings revealed current use cases such as generating code and improving text, while future desires centered around automation, data integration, and personalized assistance. The paper also discusses the implications for designing LLM-based systems for knowledge workers, emphasizing the need to support specialized skills, integrate workflows, and facilitate worker-LLM collaboration.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the growing body of research on LLMs and their impact on knowledge work. Its strengths lie in:

*   **Empirical Grounding:** The study is based on substantial survey data from a real-world organizational setting, providing a concrete picture of LLM adoption.
*   **Longitudinal Aspect:** Conducting a follow-up survey a year later provides insights into the evolution of LLM use over time.
*   **Thematic Analysis:** The detailed thematic analysis of the survey responses generates a nuanced categorization of LLM usage patterns, capturing both current practices and future aspirations.
*   **Design Implications:** The paper translates its findings into practical design implications for LLM-based systems, offering actionable guidance for developers.
*   **Focus on workflows:** The paper recognizes the importance of the context of the LLM work being within a larger workflow, where the LLM may be individual use, but the workflow is collaborative

However, the paper has some limitations:

*   **Limited Generalizability:** The study is conducted within a single technology company, potentially limiting the generalizability of the findings to other organizations or industries. The company also had limitations on which AI technologies could be used for work purposes.
*   **Self-Reported Data:** The reliance on self-reported survey data may introduce biases or inaccuracies in the accounts of LLM usage.
*   **Lack of Causal Evidence:** The survey design does not allow for establishing causal relationships between LLM use and specific outcomes, such as productivity or job satisfaction.
*   **Depth of Analysis:** While the surveys are large, the breadth of the question makes it challenging to dive into the nuance of specific use-cases for LLMs.
*   **Rapidly evolving technology:** The LLM technology has likely evolved since the fall of 2024, so some of the "future" use cases, may be occurring now. The baseline data would still be useful for comparison with any additional LLM changes and new use in the future.

**Novelty and Significance:**

Despite these limitations, the paper offers important insights into the adoption and use of LLMs by knowledge workers. Its novelty lies in:

*   **Comprehensive View:** Providing a comprehensive view of both current and future LLM usage patterns across different adoption groups and job roles.
*   **Workflow-Centric Perspective:** Focusing on how LLM tasks fit into larger workflows and collaborative processes, addressing a critical aspect often overlooked in other studies.
*   **Practical Guidance:** Generating concrete design implications for LLM-based systems that can better support the needs of knowledge workers.

The study is significant because it provides empirical evidence to inform the development and implementation of LLM-based tools in the workplace. It helps to bridge the gap between the hype surrounding LLMs and the actual realities of their adoption and use by knowledge workers. This insight is essential for designing effective systems that can improve productivity, enhance collaboration, and empower employees in the age of AI.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Chain-of-Tools: Utilizing Massive Unseen Tools in the CoT Reasoning of Frozen Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16779v1)
- **Authors**: Mengsong Wu, Tong Zhu, Han Han, Xiang Zhang, Wenbiao Shao, Wenliang Chen
- **Abstract**: Tool learning can further broaden the usage scenarios of large language models (LLMs). However most of the existing methods either need to finetune that the model can only use tools seen in the training data, or add tool demonstrations into the prompt with lower efficiency. In this paper, we present a new Tool Learning method Chain-of-Tools. It makes full use of the powerful semantic representation capability of frozen LLMs to finish tool calling in CoT reasoning with a huge and flexible tool pool which may contain unseen tools. Especially, to validate the effectiveness of our approach in the massive unseen tool scenario, we construct a new dataset SimpleToolQuestions. We conduct experiments on two numerical reasoning benchmarks (GSM8K-XL and FuncQA) and two knowledge-based question answering benchmarks (KAMEL and SimpleToolQuestions). Experimental results show that our approach performs better than the baseline. We also identify dimensions of the model output that are critical in tool selection, enhancing the model interpretability. Our code and data are available at: https://github.com/fairyshine/Chain-of-Tools .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Chain-of-Tools" (CoTools), a novel fine-tuning based method for tool learning in large language models (LLMs). CoTools aims to efficiently utilize massive and potentially unseen tools during the Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning process without negatively impacting the LLM's inherent capabilities. The key idea is to leverage the semantic representation capabilities of frozen LLMs (where the model parameters are not updated during training) to determine when and which tools to call. The paper constructs a new dataset, SimpleToolQuestions (STQuestions), to evaluate the tool selection performance, particularly in scenarios involving massive unseen tools. Experiments are conducted on numerical reasoning (GSM8K-XL, FuncQA) and knowledge-based question answering (KAMEL, STQuestions) benchmarks, demonstrating that CoTools outperforms baselines. Furthermore, the paper identifies critical dimensions within the hidden states that influence tool selection, potentially enhancing model interpretability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses an important limitation of existing tool learning methods, namely the difficulty of handling large, unseen toolsets effectively. The CoTools approach, by leveraging the semantic representation within frozen LLMs and using a contrastive learning based retriever, offers a potentially more scalable and flexible solution compared to fine-tuning the entire model or relying solely on in-context learning.
*   **Significance:** Equipping LLMs with the ability to use a vast range of external tools is crucial for their application in real-world scenarios. If CoTools proves effective, it could contribute to the development of more capable and adaptable AI agents.
*   **Dataset Contribution:** The construction of SimpleToolQuestions (STQuestions) is valuable. This dataset specifically targets the challenge of tool selection in massive, unseen tool scenarios, which is a gap in existing benchmarks.
*   **Experimental Results:** The experimental results, although showing modest improvements, provide evidence that CoTools can outperform baselines on both numerical and knowledge-based question answering tasks. The analysis of hidden state dimensions is a promising step towards better understanding and controlling tool selection within LLMs.
*   **Methodology:** The method builds on existing architectures and introduces a novel retriever and judge that are tuned using a contrastive loss, making the technique effective and efficient.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Improvement:** The performance gains over baselines are not overwhelmingly large. The improvements, especially in the numerical reasoning tasks, are relatively small, which raises the question of whether the added complexity of CoTools is fully justified in those domains.
*   **Limited novelty:** While the architecture appears novel, there appears to be a significant amount of overlap between existing works. Namely, contrastive training and tool retriever based techniques are nothing new, meaning the only novelty really comes from the addition of a 'Judge' and the application of existing methods to tool selection.

*   **Complexity and Training Resources:** While the frozen nature is an advantage, The method requires training several modules (Tool Judge, Query Encoder, Tool Encoder), meaning more resources than in-context learning.

*   **Generalizability:** The benchmarks used, although standard, are still somewhat limited in scope. It's unclear how well CoTools would generalize to more complex, real-world tasks with even larger and more diverse toolsets.
*   **Interpretability limitations:** While the analysis of hidden state dimensions is a good starting point, the actual connection between those dimensions and specific tool characteristics remains somewhat unclear. A deeper investigation would be needed to make strong claims about improved interpretability.

**Overall Assessment:**

CoTools presents a reasonable contribution to the field of tool learning, offering a new approach for effectively using massive, unseen tools with LLMs. While the improvements over baselines are not dramatic, the method addresses a critical limitation of existing approaches and provides a new benchmark for further research. The paper has the potential to influence the development of more capable and adaptable AI agents. However, the incremental improvement in performance and the need for stronger real-world validation warrants a lower rating.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Does Chain-of-Thought Reasoning Help Mobile GUI Agent? An Empirical Study
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16788v1)
- **Authors**: Li Zhang, Longxi Gao, Mengwei Xu
- **Abstract**: Reasoning capabilities have significantly improved the performance of vision-language models (VLMs) in domains such as mathematical problem-solving, coding, and visual question-answering. However, their impact on real-world applications remains unclear. This paper presents the first empirical study on the effectiveness of reasoning-enabled VLMs in mobile GUI agents, a domain that requires interpreting complex screen layouts, understanding user instructions, and executing multi-turn interactions. We evaluate two pairs of commercial models--Gemini 2.0 Flash and Claude 3.7 Sonnet--comparing their base and reasoning-enhanced versions across two static benchmarks (ScreenSpot and AndroidControl) and one interactive environment (AndroidWorld). We surprisingly find the Claude 3.7 Sonnet reasoning model achieves state-of-the-art performance on AndroidWorld. However, reasoning VLMs generally offer marginal improvements over non-reasoning models on static benchmarks and even degrade performance in some agent setups. Notably, reasoning and non-reasoning VLMs fail on different sets of tasks, suggesting that reasoning does have an impact, but its benefits and drawbacks counterbalance each other. We attribute these inconsistencies to the limitations of benchmarks and VLMs. Based on the findings, we provide insights for further enhancing mobile GUI agents in terms of benchmarks, VLMs, and their adaptability in dynamically invoking reasoning VLMs. The experimental data are publicly available at https://github.com/LlamaTouch/VLM-Reasoning-Traces.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents an empirical study investigating whether chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning enhances the performance of vision-language models (VLMs) when used as mobile GUI agents.  The authors compare commercial models (Gemini 2.0 Flash and Claude 3.7 Sonnet), with and without reasoning capabilities, across static benchmarks (ScreenSpot and AndroidControl) and a dynamic interactive environment (AndroidWorld). Surprisingly, they find that while the Claude 3.7 Sonnet reasoning model achieves state-of-the-art performance in AndroidWorld, reasoning VLMs generally offer marginal improvements or even degrade performance on static benchmarks. The paper highlights that reasoning and non-reasoning VLMs often fail on different sets of tasks, suggesting that reasoning *does* have an impact, but its benefits can be counterbalanced by drawbacks. The authors attribute these inconsistencies to benchmark limitations and imperfections in VLM grounding and consistency.  They conclude by providing recommendations for improving mobile GUI agents with reasoning, focusing on benchmark selection, VLM training, and adaptive reasoning strategies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a timely and important question: Does the increased reasoning capability of newer VLMs translate into better performance in complex, real-world multimodal tasks like mobile GUI automation? While reasoning shows immense promise in various domains, it is unclear how it can help mobile GUI agents. This paper takes an important step towards addressing this issue by performing the first comprehensive, empirical evaluation of VLM reasoning capabilities in this specific domain.

**Novelty and Significance:**

*   **First Empirical Study:** The paper's primary novelty lies in being the *first* empirical evaluation of commercial VLMs' reasoning capabilities in the context of mobile GUI agents. This fills a critical gap in understanding the practical benefits and limitations of these powerful models in a complex, real-world application.
*   **Surprising Findings:** The counterintuitive finding that reasoning VLMs don't always improve (and can sometimes degrade) performance is significant. This challenges the assumption that increased reasoning always equates to better performance and highlights the need for nuanced investigation. This result provides valuable insights into the limitations of both the models and existing benchmarks.
*   **Error Analysis:** The paper's detailed error analysis, categorizing errors by source (benchmark vs. VLM), is valuable. This provides insights into the underlying causes of performance discrepancies and informs directions for future research.
*   **Practical Implications:** The paper's suggested implications for improving mobile GUI agents by fully unleashing the reasoning capabilities of VLMs are immediately actionable, focusing on benchmark suitability, VLM training needs, and the importance of adaptive reasoning to better target task comprehension.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Model Scope:** While the choice of Gemini 2.0 Flash and Claude 3.7 Sonnet is reasonable, the study is limited in scope to only two model families. Exploring other models, especially open-source ones (as suggested in future work), would broaden the applicability of the findings.
*   **Benchmark Dependence:** The strong dependence on benchmark characteristics is a both a finding and a weakness. While the paper highlights this limitation, more could have been done to proactively design experiments that are less susceptible to benchmark biases.
*   **Lack of Deeper Insight:** While the paper identifies specific failure scenarios, it only provides a general explanation of potential reasons for each failure. It would have been beneficial to go into detail for a select number of tasks by creating use case examples, thus allowing for a more nuanced, detailed exploration of reasons as to why a task failed.

**Overall Assessment:**

Despite the limitations, the paper makes a valuable contribution by providing an initial, rigorous assessment of reasoning VLMs in mobile GUI agents. The unexpected findings and detailed error analysis have significant implications for future research and development in this area. This first step calls for further research to be conducted in the future.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper deserves a 7.5 because it makes a novel and well-executed contribution to the field. Its significance lies in the fact that it is the first empirical study on the impact of reasoning VLMs in the important application of mobile GUI agents. However, the scope is somewhat limited by the choice of models and benchmarks, and deeper insights are needed into specific failure mechanisms to push the boundaries. Future work on model diversity and fine-grained error decomposition would substantially increase the value of the paper to the community.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Auto-Regressive Diffusion for Generating 3D Human-Object Interactions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16801v1)
- **Authors**: Zichen Geng, Zeeshan Hayder, Wei Liu, Ajmal Saeed Mian
- **Abstract**: Text-driven Human-Object Interaction (Text-to-HOI) generation is an emerging field with applications in animation, video games, virtual reality, and robotics. A key challenge in HOI generation is maintaining interaction consistency in long sequences. Existing Text-to-Motion-based approaches, such as discrete motion tokenization, cannot be directly applied to HOI generation due to limited data in this domain and the complexity of the modality. To address the problem of interaction consistency in long sequences, we propose an autoregressive diffusion model (ARDHOI) that predicts the next continuous token. Specifically, we introduce a Contrastive Variational Autoencoder (cVAE) to learn a physically plausible space of continuous HOI tokens, thereby ensuring that generated human-object motions are realistic and natural. For generating sequences autoregressively, we develop a Mamba-based context encoder to capture and maintain consistent sequential actions. Additionally, we implement an MLP-based denoiser to generate the subsequent token conditioned on the encoded context. Our model has been evaluated on the OMOMO and BEHAVE datasets, where it outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods in terms of both performance and inference speed. This makes ARDHOI a robust and efficient solution for text-driven HOI tasks
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Auto-Regressive Diffusion for Generating 3D Human-Object Interactions":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ARDHOI, an autoregressive diffusion model for generating 3D human-object interaction (HOI) sequences from text descriptions. The key contributions are: (1) A Contrastive Variational Autoencoder (CVAE) that learns a physically plausible and contact-aware continuous latent space for HOI tokens, (2) a Mamba-based context encoder to capture long-range dependencies and maintain consistency in generated sequences, and (3) an MLP-based denoiser for efficient and accurate token prediction during the reverse diffusion process.  The model outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods in both accuracy and inference speed on OMOMO and BEHAVE datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addressing a Key Challenge:** The paper tackles the important and challenging problem of maintaining consistency in long-sequence HOI generation, which is crucial for realistic and useful applications.
*   **Novel Architecture:** The combination of a contrastive VAE for learning a meaningful latent space and a Mamba-based autoregressive diffusion model is a novel approach.
*   **Mamba for Efficiency:** The use of Mamba instead of Transformers for sequence encoding is a significant contribution, promising better efficiency and reduced computational costs, which are critical for long sequence generation.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper demonstrates clear quantitative improvements over existing methods on established benchmarks. The FID reduction on the OMOMO dataset is particularly noteworthy.
*   **Contrastive VAE Design:** The contrastive VAE's mechanism for distinguishing between plausible and implausible HOI tokens is a strong aspect. This is particularly relevant because many generated animations using existing methods are not physically realistic.
*   **Fast Inference:** By using Mamba and an MLP denoiser, the authors achieved fast inference speeds, a critical advantage for interactive applications.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset Limitations:** The OMOMO and BEHAVE datasets, while standard, are relatively small and may not fully capture the complexity of real-world HOI. Generalization to more diverse and complex interactions remains a question.
*   **Dependency on Initial State:** As highlighted in the paper itself, the performance is sensitive to the initial state. This limits the model's usability in scenarios where the initial state is unknown or imprecise.
*   **Qualitative Evaluation:**  While quantitative results are impressive, further qualitative analysis (beyond Fig 4.) would be beneficial.  For instance, a deeper investigation of the physically realistic/implausible HOIs generated by the model would strengthen the analysis. A user study might be helpful.
*   **Lack of Ablation with State-Of-The-Art Text-to-Motion Architectures:**  The paper does not compare directly with current state-of-the-art text-to-motion architectures (outside of HOI-specific ones), which limits the analysis of the proposed improvements. Since the core goal is text-to-motion generation involving interaction, a comparison with a strong T2M architecture would strengthen claims of improvements.
*   **Implicit Discriminator:** The term "implicit discriminator" applied to the CVAE might be a stretch. While the contrastive loss helps discriminate, a true discriminator typically involves a more direct adversarial training process.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper presents a novel architecture that effectively addresses the challenge of generating consistent and realistic 3D HOI sequences from text. The use of Mamba and contrastive VAEs is innovative.  The achieved improvements in both accuracy and efficiency are important contributions to the field. The work has the potential to influence future research in HOI generation, animation, robotics, and virtual reality. The limitations of the benchmark datasets and sensitivity to initial conditions are important considerations for future research.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper demonstrates a clear advance in the field of text-to-HOI generation. The architecture is novel and performs well, and the focus on efficiency makes it a promising approach.  While the limitations concerning dataset diversity and sensitivity to the initial state are important considerations, and the comparison with state-of-the-art T2M models is missing, the overall contribution is significant. It pushes the boundaries of HOI generation and provides a robust and efficient solution.  Therefore a score of 7 is appropriate.  A score above 7 would require either a more thorough exploration of the limitations or a more dramatic improvement in performance, or a more direct comparison with a broader range of current state-of-the-art motion generation techniques.

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 7/10

### When Debate Fails: Bias Reinforcement in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16814v1)
- **Authors**: Jihwan Oh, Minchan Jeong, Jongwoo Ko, Se-Young Yun
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models $($LLMs$)$ solve complex problems using training-free methods like prompt engineering and in-context learning, yet ensuring reasoning correctness remains challenging. While self-correction methods such as self-consistency and self-refinement aim to improve reliability, they often reinforce biases due to the lack of effective feedback mechanisms. Multi-Agent Debate $($MAD$)$ has emerged as an alternative, but we identify two key limitations: bias reinforcement, where debate amplifies model biases instead of correcting them, and lack of perspective diversity, as all agents share the same model and reasoning patterns, limiting true debate effectiveness. To systematically evaluate these issues, we introduce $\textit{MetaNIM Arena}$, a benchmark designed to assess LLMs in adversarial strategic decision-making, where dynamic interactions influence optimal decisions. To overcome MAD's limitations, we propose $\textbf{DReaMAD}$ $($$\textbf{D}$iverse $\textbf{Rea}$soning via $\textbf{M}$ulti-$\textbf{A}$gent $\textbf{D}$ebate with Refined Prompt$)$, a novel framework that $(1)$ refines LLM's strategic prior knowledge to improve reasoning quality and $(2)$ promotes diverse viewpoints within a single model by systematically modifying prompts, reducing bias. Empirical results show that $\textbf{DReaMAD}$ significantly improves decision accuracy, reasoning diversity, and bias mitigation across multiple strategic tasks, establishing it as a more effective approach for LLM-based decision-making.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper identifies and addresses the problem of bias reinforcement in Large Language Models (LLMs) when using multi-agent debate (MAD) for strategic decision-making.  The authors argue that MAD, while intended to improve reasoning through diverse perspectives, can actually amplify existing biases due to a lack of true diversity among the agents (typically instantiations of the same model) and a tendency to fixate on initial, possibly flawed, reasoning patterns.  To address this, they introduce MetaNIM Arena, a new benchmark for evaluating LLMs in adversarial strategic settings, and propose DReaMAD (Diverse Reasoning via Multi-Agent Debate with Refined Prompt), a framework that aims to refine LLMs' strategic prior knowledge and promote diverse viewpoints within a single model using structured self-prompt refinement and perspective diversification. Empirical results demonstrate that DReaMAD improves decision accuracy, reasoning diversity, and bias mitigation across several strategic tasks compared to standard prompting and existing self-correction methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the identification and framing of the "bias reinforcement" problem in MAD within the context of strategic decision-making. While MAD is an established technique, the paper makes a compelling argument that it is not a panacea and can even be detrimental in certain situations.  The introduction of MetaNIM Arena is also a significant contribution, providing a controlled environment for rigorously evaluating strategic reasoning in LLMs, a gap in existing benchmarks.  The DReaMAD framework itself is a novel approach that combines prior knowledge elicitation with a systematic approach to prompt diversification, offering a practical solution to the identified limitations of MAD.
    However, the combination of structured prompting, diversity enhancement, and debate draws inspiration from existing approaches which could be argued as combining existing works.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work is substantial for several reasons:
    *   It raises important concerns about the use of MAD in LLMs, highlighting potential pitfalls and challenging the assumption that simply increasing the number of agents leads to better reasoning.
    *   MetaNIM Arena provides a valuable tool for the community to better understand and evaluate LLMs' strategic reasoning abilities.
    *   DReaMAD offers a practical and effective approach for mitigating bias reinforcement and promoting diversity in LLM-based decision-making, potentially leading to more robust and reliable AI systems.
    *   The findings have broader implications for the design of self-correction mechanisms in LLMs, emphasizing the need for methods that actively challenge and diversify reasoning patterns rather than simply reinforcing existing ones.
    *   The use of combinatorial game theory is an interesting approach. This gives a certain mathematical rigour to evaluation. However, the combinatorial games are also quite simplistic, raising questions about the generalizability of findings.
    *   There is no analysis or evidence that explains why the chosen prompt strategies are selected.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and well-motivated research questions.
    *   Rigorous experimental methodology with a new benchmark and thorough comparisons to existing methods.
    *   The theoretical insights are well connected to their implementation in DReaMAD.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation with multiple models and strategic tasks.
    *   The writing is generally clear and well-organized.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The scope of the experiments is limited to a specific set of combinatorial games in MetaNIM Arena. The extent to which the findings generalize to other strategic domains (e.g., negotiation, game playing) or real-world decision-making scenarios is not fully explored.
    *   While the paper addresses diversity of *perspectives*, the diverse agent always relies on single LLM, this could limits LLM's diversity.
    *   The mechanism on the initial LLM self-prompt refinement in DReaMAD is still unclear and relies heavily on prompt engineering.
    *   The authors could provide a more thorough theoretical explanation for how the chosen strategies work to combat bias and what are the limitations of these specific choices.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper is likely to influence the design and evaluation of self-correction and debate-based methods in LLMs, pushing the field towards more robust and diverse reasoning techniques.  It could also inspire further research on developing more comprehensive benchmarks for strategic decision-making and on understanding the factors that contribute to bias reinforcement in LLMs.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the above points, I am assigning a score of **7**. The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by identifying and addressing the bias reinforcement problem in MAD, introducing a novel benchmark, and proposing a practical solution in DReaMAD. The experimental results are compelling and demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach. However, the limitations in the scope of the experiments and the reliance of DReaMAD on specific prompt strategies prevent a higher score. Moreover, while inspired, the combination of techniques is not completely revolutionary. The paper's impact could be strengthened by exploring broader applications of DReaMAD and providing more theoretical justification for its design choices.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### When Tom Eats Kimchi: Evaluating Cultural Bias of Multimodal Large Language Models in Cultural Mixture Contexts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16826v1)
- **Authors**: Jun Seong Kim, Kyaw Ye Thu, Javad Ismayilzada, Junyeong Park, Eunsu Kim, Huzama Ahmad, Na Min An, James Thorne, Alice Oh
- **Abstract**: In a highly globalized world, it is important for multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) to recognize and respond correctly to mixed-cultural inputs. For example, a model should correctly identify kimchi (Korean food) in an image both when an Asian woman is eating it, as well as an African man is eating it. However, current MLLMs show an over-reliance on the visual features of the person, leading to misclassification of the entities. To examine the robustness of MLLMs to different ethnicity, we introduce MixCuBe, a cross-cultural bias benchmark, and study elements from five countries and four ethnicities. Our findings reveal that MLLMs achieve both higher accuracy and lower sensitivity to such perturbation for high-resource cultures, but not for low-resource cultures. GPT-4o, the best-performing model overall, shows up to 58% difference in accuracy between the original and perturbed cultural settings in low-resource cultures. Our dataset is publicly available at: https://huggingface.co/datasets/kyawyethu/MixCuBe.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "When Tom Eats Kimchi: Evaluating Cultural Bias of Multimodal Large Language Models in Cultural Mixture Contexts":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the cultural bias of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in identifying cultural markers when presented with people of different ethnicities. The authors introduce MIXCUBE, a new benchmark dataset with images of food, festivals, and clothing from five cultures (Azerbaijan, Myanmar, South Korea, UK, and US) where the ethnicity of the person in the image is synthetically altered. They evaluate three MLLMs (GPT-4o, GLM-4v, and InternVL2.5) on tasks of Country Identification and Cultural Marker Identification. The results show that MLLMs perform worse when the ethnicity of the person in the image doesn't align with the culture of the marker (e.g., an African person eating Korean kimchi). The bias is more pronounced for low-resource cultures like Myanmar and Azerbaijan.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a worthwhile contribution by specifically examining cultural biases in MLLMs within *mixed cultural contexts*. Existing work often focuses on either culture-specific or general multicultural settings, but the nuance of interweaving ethnicity and cultural artifacts is relatively novel. The construction of MIXCUBE to specifically test this bias is also a significant effort.
*   **Significance:** The issue of cultural bias in AI systems is increasingly important. Identifying and mitigating these biases is crucial for fairness and preventing harmful stereotypes. By showing that MLLMs are sensitive to ethnicity in cultural recognition tasks, the paper highlights a concrete area where these models need improvement. The use of synthetic images to systematically test these biases is a sound methodological approach.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly defines the problem of cultural bias in mixed cultural contexts and provides a measurable framework.
    *   **Thorough data generation:** The generation of the MIXCUBE dataset seems well-executed, with careful attention to quality assurance and masking procedures. The inclusion of low, medium, and high-resource cultures allows for interesting comparative analysis.
    *   **Rigorous evaluation:** The evaluation is comprehensive, using different MLLMs and tasks, and employing a secondary LLM for label evaluation. The analysis of accuracy differences is clearly presented using heatmaps.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The paper makes the dataset publicly available.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited number of cultures and ethnicities:** While the five cultures and four ethnicities are a good start, a broader range would strengthen the generalizability of the findings. The ethnicities are rather broad and could be more specific.
    *   **Synthetic images limitations:** Synthetic images can introduce artifacts, as the authors acknowledge, and might not fully capture the complexity of real-world scenarios. This reliance on generated images introduces a degree of artificiality that should be taken into account when interpreting results.
    *   **Generalizability to other tasks:** The focus on food, festivals, and clothing, while practical, raises questions about whether similar biases exist in other areas where MLLMs are deployed (e.g., medical diagnosis, legal analysis). Further investigation may be necessary to better understand the breadth and depth of this bias across diverse applications.
    *   **Mitigation Strategies:** While highlighting the issue, the paper doesn't delve deeply into potential mitigation strategies. It would be helpful to discuss approaches for reducing the observed biases in MLLMs, such as fine-tuning with diverse datasets or adjusting model architectures.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research by:
    *   Encouraging more work on cultural bias in MLLMs beyond standard VQA.
    *   Providing a benchmark for evaluating the cultural awareness of MLLMs in mixed contexts.
    *   Inspiring the development of new methods for mitigating cultural biases in AI systems.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by identifying and quantifying a specific type of cultural bias in MLLMs. While limited in scope and reliant on synthetic data, the study is well-executed and highlights an important area for future research. The release of the MIXCUBE dataset is a significant asset for the community.

Score: 7

**Justification:** The paper is novel and contributes to an increasingly important area of research. The dataset and analysis are valuable, but the limitations in the range of cultures/ethnicities and the reliance on synthetic images prevent a higher score. The paper's impact will depend on how widely the dataset is adopted and whether it spurs further research into bias mitigation strategies.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Safe and Reliable Diffusion Models via Subspace Projection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16835v1)
- **Authors**: Huiqiang Chen, Tianqing Zhu, Linlin Wang, Xin Yu, Longxiang Gao, Wanlei Zhou
- **Abstract**: Large-scale text-to-image (T2I) diffusion models have revolutionized image generation, enabling the synthesis of highly detailed visuals from textual descriptions. However, these models may inadvertently generate inappropriate content, such as copyrighted works or offensive images. While existing methods attempt to eliminate specific unwanted concepts, they often fail to ensure complete removal, allowing the concept to reappear in subtle forms. For instance, a model may successfully avoid generating images in Van Gogh's style when explicitly prompted with 'Van Gogh', yet still reproduce his signature artwork when given the prompt 'Starry Night'. In this paper, we propose SAFER, a novel and efficient approach for thoroughly removing target concepts from diffusion models. At a high level, SAFER is inspired by the observed low-dimensional structure of the text embedding space. The method first identifies a concept-specific subspace $S_c$ associated with the target concept c. It then projects the prompt embeddings onto the complementary subspace of $S_c$, effectively erasing the concept from the generated images. Since concepts can be abstract and difficult to fully capture using natural language alone, we employ textual inversion to learn an optimized embedding of the target concept from a reference image. This enables more precise subspace estimation and enhances removal performance. Furthermore, we introduce a subspace expansion strategy to ensure comprehensive and robust concept erasure. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SAFER consistently and effectively erases unwanted concepts from diffusion models while preserving generation quality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper "Safe and Reliable Diffusion Models via Subspace Projection" proposes a novel approach called SAFER to remove unwanted concepts (like artistic styles, objects, or nudity) from text-to-image diffusion models. SAFER leverages the low-dimensional structure of the text embedding space. It identifies a concept-specific subspace, projects prompt embeddings onto its complement to erase the concept, and uses textual inversion to learn optimized embeddings from reference images for more precise subspace estimation. A subspace expansion strategy is also employed for comprehensive concept erasure. Experiments demonstrate that SAFER effectively removes unwanted concepts while preserving generation quality, outperforming existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel and well-motivated approach to concept erasure in diffusion models. The key novelties are:
    *   **Subspace Projection:**  The idea of identifying and projecting onto the complementary subspace is a good abstraction for the problem of concept removal.
    *   **Textual Inversion Enhancement:** The use of textual inversion to learn concept embeddings from reference images is a clever way to address the limitations of natural language for defining complex concepts. This allows SAFER to handle abstract styles and other concepts that are difficult to define precisely through language alone.
    *   **Subspace Expansion:** The progressive subspace expansion strategy adds robustness and completeness to the erasure process. This addresses the issue where single textual prompts might not capture the entirety of a concept.

*   **Significance:**  The problem of generating inappropriate or harmful content is a significant concern with the increasing accessibility of diffusion models. SAFER provides a potentially valuable tool for mitigating these risks. The ability to effectively remove copyrighted artistic styles or prevent the generation of nudity-related content can contribute to safer and more trustworthy image generation systems.  The clear experimental results showing SAFER's superior performance compared to SOTA baselines further underscore its significance. The method is also efficient, requiring no additional training. This can increase the likelihood of its adoption.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-Motivated:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of concept erasure and the limitations of existing methods.
    *   **Technically Sound:** The proposed method is well-explained and theoretically grounded. The mathematical formulations are clear and the implementation details are well-documented.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments cover a range of concepts (artistic styles, objects, nudity) and compare SAFER against multiple strong baselines, demonstrating its effectiveness. Both qualitative and quantitative results are provided. Ablation study clarifies the impact of the different components in SAFER.
    *   **Practicality:** The efficiency of SAFER and ability to handle multiple concepts without additional training increases practicality.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependence on Reference Images:** Although textual inversion is used to improve concept capture, the method still relies on curated reference images, which can affect the erasure result. The quality and coverage of reference images will significantly impact the accuracy and robustness of the extracted concept subspace and, ultimately, the effectiveness of SAFER.
    *   **Potential for Over-Erasure:** While the paper claims to preserve generation quality, aggressive projection could potentially lead to unintended removal of related or associated concepts. This is not explored or evaluated. The parameters used for subspace expansion also need careful tuning to avoid both incomplete removal and over-erasure.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Prompting:** Further research could focus on how prompt engineering interacts with the effectiveness of SAFER. It's possible that certain prompts might be more or less susceptible to concept leakage even after applying SAFER. While they use ChatGPT to create a set of diverse prompt, they could further explore prompt engineering around that diverse set.

*   **Potential Influence:** The SAFER method has the potential to influence future research in concept erasure for diffusion models. The subspace projection approach, combined with textual inversion and subspace expansion, provides a strong framework for developing more robust and reliable safety mechanisms. It could be a component in larger systems aimed at ensuring the ethical and responsible use of image generation technologies.

**Justification of Score:**

Overall, this is a strong paper with significant novelty and potential impact. While some weaknesses exist, the proposed approach is well-motivated, technically sound, and comprehensively evaluated. The paper represents a valuable contribution to the field of trustworthy machine learning and could have a positive influence on the development of safer and more controllable image generation systems. The issues about reference images and potential for over-erasure are valid concerns that need further research, preventing a higher score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### LoRASculpt: Sculpting LoRA for Harmonizing General and Specialized Knowledge in Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16843v1)
- **Authors**: Jian Liang, Wenke Huang, Guancheng Wan, Qu Yang, Mang Ye
- **Abstract**: While Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) excel at generalizing across modalities and tasks, effectively adapting them to specific downstream tasks while simultaneously retaining both general and specialized knowledge remains challenging. Although Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is widely used to efficiently acquire specialized knowledge in MLLMs, it introduces substantial harmful redundancy during visual instruction tuning, which exacerbates the forgetting of general knowledge and degrades downstream task performance. To address this issue, we propose LoRASculpt to eliminate harmful redundant parameters, thereby harmonizing general and specialized knowledge. Specifically, under theoretical guarantees, we introduce sparse updates into LoRA to discard redundant parameters effectively. Furthermore, we propose a Conflict Mitigation Regularizer to refine the update trajectory of LoRA, mitigating knowledge conflicts with the pretrained weights. Extensive experimental results demonstrate that even at very high degree of sparsity ($\le$ 5%), our method simultaneously enhances generalization and downstream task performance. This confirms that our approach effectively mitigates the catastrophic forgetting issue and further promotes knowledge harmonization in MLLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "LoRASculpt," a novel framework designed to address the challenge of balancing general knowledge retention and specialized task adaptation in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) fine-tuned using LoRA. The authors argue that LoRA introduces harmful redundancy which exacerbates catastrophic forgetting and degrades downstream task performance. LoRASculpt mitigates this by: (1) introducing sparse updates into LoRA during training to eliminate redundant parameters (Sparcifying LoRA) and (2) employing a conflict mitigation regularizer to refine the LoRA update trajectory, minimizing conflicts with pre-trained weights (Regularizing LoRA). The paper provides theoretical guarantees for the sparsification approach and showcases experimental results demonstrating simultaneous improvements in generalization and downstream task performance, even at high sparsity levels.  The method is also adapted for the connector module within MLLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to address the forgetting issue in MLLMs fine-tuned with LoRA. While the idea of pruning for efficiency and mitigating forgetting is not entirely new, the way the authors integrate sparse updates directly into LoRA training, along with the theoretical guarantees for sparsity, offers a distinct contribution. The conflict mitigation regularizer is also a novel component, aimed at steering LoRA updates towards less critical regions of the pre-trained model.  The explicit focus on the interaction between sparsification and knowledge conflict in LoRA, and the combination of these elements, are novel aspects.  The tailoring of the method for the connector module within MLLMs is also a small but significant addition.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical issue in the MLLM space: the trade-off between task-specific adaptation and the retention of general knowledge. By mitigating forgetting, LoRASculpt can potentially improve the practical applicability and reliability of MLLMs across diverse tasks.  The experimental results, demonstrating simultaneous improvements in generalization and downstream performance, support the significance of the approach. The detailed ablation studies and diagnostic analysis provide insights into the effectiveness of each component. The finding that even the connector module experiences forgetting is a valuable observation.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Theoretical Guarantees:** The paper offers theoretical support for its sparsification approach, providing an upper bound on the probability of exceeding the expected sparsity.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:**  The authors conduct extensive experiments on VQA and Captioning tasks, comparing LoRASculpt against several state-of-the-art baselines, which enhances the trustworthiness of the results.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies demonstrate the individual contributions of the sparsifying and regularizing components of LoRASculpt.
    *   **Diagnostic Analysis:** The analysis of catastrophic forgetting in the connector module and the detailed hyperparameter studies provide deeper insights into the method's behavior.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and provides a clear explanation of the proposed method and its underlying motivation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:**  The theoretical analysis, while adding rigor, may be difficult for some practitioners to fully grasp.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:**  Like many machine learning methods, LoRASculpt likely has hyperparameters that require careful tuning. While the authors provide a hyperparameter study, there's always a risk that the optimal hyperparameter settings could vary for different datasets and model architectures.
    *   **Limited Generalizability Experiments:** While the upstream evaluation shows some generalisation gains, further experiments on more challenging generalisation tasks would reinforce the paper's claims more strongly.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** The actual improvements in downstream task performance, while consistent, might be considered incremental in some cases. The theoretical guarantees are a better selling point than the absolute performance gain.

*   **Potential Impact:**  LoRASculpt has the potential to influence future research in parameter-efficient fine-tuning of MLLMs. The idea of actively sculpting LoRA to reduce redundancy and mitigate knowledge conflict could inspire other researchers to explore similar approaches. The analysis of forgetting in the connector module may also prompt more research into this often-overlooked aspect of MLLM design.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a well-motivated, theoretically grounded, and experimentally validated approach to address an important problem in MLLM fine-tuning. LoRASculpt offers a novel combination of sparsification and regularization techniques, resulting in simultaneous improvements in generalization and downstream task performance. While the performance gains are somewhat incremental, the theoretical guarantees and comprehensive evaluation add significant value to the research. Therefore, a score of 7 is warranted. The paper is a solid contribution that advances the field, but further research will be needed to fully assess its long-term impact and generalizability.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards LLM Guardrails via Sparse Representation Steering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16851v1)
- **Authors**: Zeqing He, Zhibo Wang, Huiyu Xu, Kui Ren
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance in natural language generation tasks, yet their uncontrolled outputs pose significant ethical and safety risks. Recently, representation engineering methods have shown promising results in steering model behavior by modifying the rich semantic information encoded in activation vectors. However, due to the difficulty of precisely disentangling semantic directions within high-dimensional representation space, existing approaches suffer from three major limitations: lack of fine-grained control, quality degradation of generated content, and poor interpretability. To address these challenges, we propose a sparse encoding-based representation engineering method, named SRE, which decomposes polysemantic activations into a structured, monosemantic feature space. By leveraging sparse autoencoding, our approach isolates and adjusts only task-specific sparse feature dimensions, enabling precise and interpretable steering of model behavior while preserving content quality. We validate our method on three critical domains, i.e., safety, fairness, and truthfulness using the open-source LLM Gemma-2-2B-it. Experimental results show that SRE achieves superior controllability while maintaining the overall quality of generated content (i.e., controllability and quality), demonstrating its effectiveness as a fine-grained and interpretable activation steering framework.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SRE (Sparse Representation Steering), a novel method for guiding the behavior of Large Language Models (LLMs) by manipulating their internal representations. SRE tackles limitations of existing representation engineering techniques by decomposing activations into a structured, monosemantic feature space using a sparse autoencoder. This allows for fine-grained control over specific tasks (e.g., safety, fairness, truthfulness) while minimizing unintended impacts on content quality and interpretability. The method is evaluated on the open-source LLM Gemma-2-2B-it, demonstrating superior controllability and quality of generated content compared to existing steering techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The core novelty lies in the combination of sparse autoencoders with representation engineering for LLMs.  Existing representation engineering techniques often struggle with disentangling semantic directions in high-dimensional spaces, leading to coarse-grained control and potential content degradation. Sparse autoencoders offer a way to decompose activations into more interpretable and manageable components. Applying this to LLM steering appears to be a significant step forward, offering a more granular and targeted approach. Prior works either used more direct manipulation of activations or relied on techniques like PCA, which don't inherently provide the same degree of disentanglement. The paper improves upon previous methods by making it possible to selectively intervene with the most relevant features, which increases the model’s steering capabilities.

* **Significance:**  The ability to control LLM behavior is a crucial area of research, particularly for ensuring safety, fairness, and trustworthiness. SRE directly addresses the practical challenges in this area by offering a method that improves both controllability and content quality.  If the experimental results are robust and generalize well to other models and tasks, SRE could become a valuable tool for mitigating risks associated with LLM deployment.  The interpretability aspect is also significant, as it allows researchers and practitioners to understand *why* a specific intervention is working, potentially leading to more robust and reliable steering strategies. The claim to superior controllability and content quality further enhances its significance by proving it improves upon existing methods in these key areas.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing representation engineering methods.
    * **Well-Motivated Approach:** The use of sparse autoencoders is well-justified and aligns with the goal of disentangling semantic information.
    * **Strong Experimental Results:**  The experimental results, particularly the comparison against baselines like ActAdd and CAA, demonstrate the effectiveness of SRE across multiple domains.
    * **Focus on Interpretability:**  The emphasis on creating interpretable steering vectors is a notable strength.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Model Dependency:** The method's reliance on Gemma-2-2B-it and its corresponding sparse autoencoder (GemmaScope) is a limitation.  While this is understandable for a research paper, it raises questions about the generalizability of SRE to other LLMs for which pre-trained sparse autoencoders may not be available or easily trained. How well does this approach scale up to larger models with different architectures?
    * **Computational Overhead:** Although mentioned as a limitation, it is important to determine the extent of the increased computational costs associated with SRE compared to simpler steering methods. This may impact its practicality in real-time or resource-constrained environments.
    * **Prompt Sensitivity:** The method relies on well-structured positive and negative prompt pairs. The difficulty in constructing such pairs for novel or ill-defined tasks can limit SRE's generalizability, as acknowledged by the authors. Could the construction of such pairs be automated to a degree?
    * **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** The paper primarily focuses on empirical results. A deeper theoretical analysis of why sparse representations are effective for steering would strengthen the contribution. More formal analysis of the convergence properties of the steering process would also be beneficial.

* **Potential Influence:**
    * SRE could inspire further research into the application of sparse representation learning to LLM control.
    * The method could be adopted by practitioners seeking more fine-grained and interpretable ways to steer LLM behavior.
    * The insights gained from analyzing sparse steering vectors could lead to a better understanding of how LLMs encode semantic information.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to LLM steering with significant potential for impact. The experimental results are compelling, and the focus on interpretability is commendable. However, the limitations related to model dependency, computational overhead, and prompt sensitivity prevent it from achieving a truly exceptional score. Further research is needed to address these limitations and demonstrate the broader applicability of SRE.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### MMCR: Benchmarking Cross-Source Reasoning in Scientific Papers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16856v1)
- **Authors**: Yang Tian, Zheng Lu, Mingqi Gao, Zheng Liu, Bo Zhao
- **Abstract**: Fully comprehending scientific papers by machines reflects a high level of Artificial General Intelligence, requiring the ability to reason across fragmented and heterogeneous sources of information, presenting a complex and practically significant challenge. While Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have made remarkable strides in various tasks, particularly those involving reasoning with evidence source from single image or text page, their ability to use cross-source information for reasoning remains an open problem. This work presents MMCR, a high-difficulty benchmark designed to evaluate VLMs' capacity for reasoning with cross-source information from scientific papers. The benchmark comprises 276 high-quality questions, meticulously annotated by humans across 7 subjects and 10 task types. Experiments with 18 VLMs demonstrate that cross-source reasoning presents a substantial challenge for existing models. Notably, even the top-performing model, GPT-4o, achieved only 48.55% overall accuracy, with only 20% accuracy in multi-table comprehension tasks, while the second-best model, Qwen2.5-VL-72B, reached 39.86% overall accuracy. Furthermore, we investigated the impact of the Chain-of-Thought (CoT) technique on cross-source reasoning and observed a detrimental effect on small models, whereas larger models demonstrated substantially enhanced performance. These results highlight the pressing need to develop VLMs capable of effectively utilizing cross-source information for reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MMCR, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the cross-source reasoning capabilities of Vision-Language Models (VLMs) on scientific papers.  The benchmark consists of 276 high-quality questions across seven subjects and ten task types, meticulously annotated by human experts. The authors conducted experiments with 18 VLMs, demonstrating that cross-source reasoning remains a significant challenge.  They also investigated the impact of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting, finding a detrimental effect on smaller models but enhanced performance in larger models. The paper highlights the pressing need for VLMs capable of effectively utilizing cross-source information for scientific paper understanding.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:**  The paper addresses a crucial gap in the evaluation of VLMs: cross-source reasoning within the complex domain of scientific papers. Existing benchmarks often focus on single-source understanding or generate annotations using models, which can introduce bias. MMCR directly tackles these limitations through high-quality, human-annotated data. This focus on cross-source reasoning is significantly important because real-world understanding requires integration of information from multiple sources.
    *   **Significance:** The task of scientific paper understanding is inherently important for advancing AI and automating knowledge discovery.  The benchmark tackles a real-world problem with significant implications for research automation and scientific progress.
    *   **Benchmark Quality:** The authors emphasize the rigorous annotation process involving expert annotators, LLM-assisted filtering, and cross-checking. This contributes to the creation of a robust and reliable benchmark. The explicit filtering to prevent answering from internal knowledge or reasoning is also important.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates a substantial number of VLMs, including both open-source and proprietary models. It provides detailed performance breakdowns across different task types, offering valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of current models.
    *   **CoT analysis:** Exploring the effect of CoT and observing the differential impact on small vs large models is useful.

*   **Weaknesses:**
