    *   **Limited Dataset Size:** While the dataset is rigorously annotated, 276 questions across 31 papers might be considered relatively small, especially given the variability in scientific papers. While the authors mention the effort in annotating, the small size might not fully represent the space of cross-source reasoning problems.
    *   **Evaluation Metric:**  The paper relies on exact match as the primary evaluation metric.  This is a common metric, but it can be overly strict, potentially penalizing models for near-correct answers that demonstrate a good understanding of the content.  The definition of "correct" in a multi-source context could also be subject to some interpretation from an external person.
    *   **Limited Analysis of Error Types:** While the error analysis provides some insights, it could be more fine-grained and detailed. A deeper dive into the specific challenges posed by different types of cross-source reasoning (e.g., linking definitions across sections vs. integrating data from tables and figures) could be more beneficial. The provided analysis of errors might also be a source of debate as it's categorized from a manual process.
    *   **Lack of baselines with information retrieval:** The paper could be augmented by baselines using modern information retrieval (IR) models and Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) techniques.

*   **Potential Influence:** The MMCR benchmark has the potential to significantly influence the development of VLMs for scientific understanding. It provides a standardized and challenging evaluation platform that can drive innovation in areas such as cross-source reasoning, multi-modal integration, and handling complex scientific data. The benchmark could also facilitate the development of new prompting strategies and training techniques tailored to scientific paper understanding.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a solid contribution, tackling a challenging and relevant problem with a well-designed benchmark. However, the limitations regarding dataset size, evaluation metric, and depth of error analysis prevent it from being truly exceptional. The novelty is significant, and the results are valuable, but the potential impact could be amplified with a larger, more diverse dataset and more nuanced evaluation techniques. Given these considerations, a score of 7 reflects the paper's valuable contributions and potential influence, while acknowledging its limitations and areas for improvement.

**Score: 7**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### MTBench: A Multimodal Time Series Benchmark for Temporal Reasoning and Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16858v1)
- **Authors**: Jialin Chen, Aosong Feng, Ziyu Zhao, Juan Garza, Gaukhar Nurbek, Cheng Qin, Ali Maatouk, Leandros Tassiulas, Yifeng Gao, Rex Ying
- **Abstract**: Understanding the relationship between textual news and time-series evolution is a critical yet under-explored challenge in applied data science. While multimodal learning has gained traction, existing multimodal time-series datasets fall short in evaluating cross-modal reasoning and complex question answering, which are essential for capturing complex interactions between narrative information and temporal patterns. To bridge this gap, we introduce Multimodal Time Series Benchmark (MTBench), a large-scale benchmark designed to evaluate large language models (LLMs) on time series and text understanding across financial and weather domains. MTbench comprises paired time series and textual data, including financial news with corresponding stock price movements and weather reports aligned with historical temperature records. Unlike existing benchmarks that focus on isolated modalities, MTbench provides a comprehensive testbed for models to jointly reason over structured numerical trends and unstructured textual narratives. The richness of MTbench enables formulation of diverse tasks that require a deep understanding of both text and time-series data, including time-series forecasting, semantic and technical trend analysis, and news-driven question answering (QA). These tasks target the model's ability to capture temporal dependencies, extract key insights from textual context, and integrate cross-modal information. We evaluate state-of-the-art LLMs on MTbench, analyzing their effectiveness in modeling the complex relationships between news narratives and temporal patterns. Our findings reveal significant challenges in current models, including difficulties in capturing long-term dependencies, interpreting causality in financial and weather trends, and effectively fusing multimodal information.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MTBench: A Multimodal Time Series Benchmark for Temporal Reasoning and Question Answering":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MTBench, a novel multimodal time-series benchmark designed to evaluate the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to reason across both time-series data and textual narratives. The benchmark includes datasets from the financial (stock prices and news) and weather (temperature records and weather reports) domains. Unlike existing benchmarks that primarily focus on predictive tasks, MTBench includes diverse reasoning-intensive tasks like semantic trend analysis, technical indicator prediction, and news-driven question answering.  The authors evaluate several state-of-the-art LLMs on the benchmark, revealing challenges in capturing temporal dependencies, interpreting causality, and fusing multimodal information. The dataset, code, and documentation are publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper demonstrates moderate novelty. The key strength lies in explicitly targeting *reasoning* capabilities over time-series data *combined* with textual information, rather than solely focusing on prediction accuracy. While existing datasets may touch on multimodality in this space, the explicit design for tasks that require deep understanding and cross-modal reasoning sets MTBench apart. Specifically the inclusion of news-driven QA and the balanced consistent and misaligned data sets in Finance contribute novelty. The customizable dataset generation framework is also a positive addition.

**Significance:** The paper holds reasonable significance for the multimodal time-series analysis and LLM communities.  The identification of challenges faced by current LLMs on tasks that require deeper temporal reasoning and causal inference is valuable. The benchmark itself provides a concrete tool for researchers to develop and evaluate new models with improved reasoning capabilities. The public availability of the dataset and code promotes reproducibility and further research. However, the current domains (finance and weather) are relatively well-explored, and the synthetic weather reports, while a necessary workaround, might limit the real-world relevance.

**Strengths:**

*   **Focus on Reasoning:** Shifts the focus from pure prediction to more complex reasoning tasks, which are critical for real-world applications.
*   **Multimodal Integration:**  Explicitly designs tasks that require the integration of time-series data and textual narratives. The introduction of misaligned dataset is novel to assess the ability of models to identify and filter out potentially misleading data.
*   **Customizable Framework:** Provides a framework for generating datasets with controllable granularity and input lengths, enhancing flexibility.
*   **Public Availability:** The public release of the dataset, code, and documentation facilitates future research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Domains:** The benchmark currently only includes financial and weather domains. While these are valuable, expanding to other areas could increase the generalizability.
*   **Synthetic Weather Reports:** The use of LLMs to generate weather reports, while a practical solution, introduces potential biases and may not fully capture the nuances of real-world weather reporting.
*   **Reliance on Existing LLMs for Task Definition:**  The correlation prediction relies on GPT-4 for generating ground truth labels, which may have its inherent biases.
*   **Lack of comparison to non-LLM methods:** While it showcases LLM weaknesses, it doesn't evaluate traditional time series methods, and whether LLMs provide any added value compared to them.
*   **Limited Depth of Domain Expertise** While the paper makes good suggestions, it would improve the credibility to seek domain expert to validate the dataset and tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7**. The paper presents a moderately novel and reasonably significant contribution to the field. The clear focus on reasoning, the multimodal integration, and the customizable framework are valuable strengths. However, the limited scope of domains, reliance on synthetic weather reports, and a lack of direct comparison with non-LLM-based approaches slightly lower the score. The inclusion of news-driven QA makes it good enough to warrant a paper. There's room for improvement by expanding the benchmark to other domains, incorporating real-world weather reports, and exploring different modeling techniques.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL

### City2Scene: Improving Acoustic Scene Classification with City Features
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16862v1)
- **Authors**: Yiqiang Cai, Yizhou Tan, Peihong Zhang, Yuxuan Liu, Shengchen Li, Xi Shao, Mark D. Plumbley
- **Abstract**: Acoustic scene recordings are often collected from a diverse range of cities. Most existing acoustic scene classification (ASC) approaches focus on identifying common acoustic scene patterns across cities to enhance generalization. In contrast, we hypothesize that city-specific environmental and cultural differences in acoustic features are beneficial for the ASC task. In this paper, we introduce City2Scene, a novel framework that leverages city features to improve ASC. City2Scene transfers the city-specific knowledge from city classification models to a scene classification model using knowledge distillation. We evaluated City2Scene on the DCASE Challenge Task 1 datasets, where each audio clip is annotated with both scene and city labels. Experimental results demonstrate that city features provide valuable information for classifying scenes. By distilling the city-specific knowledge, City2Scene effectively improves accuracy for various state-of-the-art ASC backbone models, including both CNNs and Transformers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces City2Scene, a novel framework that leverages city-specific features to improve acoustic scene classification (ASC). The key idea is that cities possess unique environmental and cultural characteristics reflected in their acoustic scenes, and exploiting this can boost performance. City2Scene trains a city classification model and then distills the city-specific knowledge learned by this model into the training of an ASC model. This is achieved by using the city classification model to provide "soft targets" reflecting city characteristics to guide the ASC model.  The framework is evaluated on DCASE Challenge Task 1 datasets, which contain both scene and city labels. The results show consistent improvement in ASC accuracy when using City2Scene with various backbone models (CNNs and Transformers) and city teacher ensembles, without increasing inference complexity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of leveraging city-specific information to improve ASC is relatively novel. While prior work has addressed domain shift across cities, it has primarily focused on disentanglement or ignoring city characteristics. City2Scene attempts to use these city-specific characteristics as features which enhances ASC performance. The knowledge distillation approach is a well-established technique, but the specific application to transfer city knowledge to scene classification seems to be a novel application of distillation framework.

*   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates that city features do provide valuable information for distinguishing scenes, as the introduction of the city-to-scene soft targets improved accuracy across the baseline methods. The consistent performance improvements across multiple datasets and backbone models suggest that City2Scene is a generalizable and effective approach. Achieving state-of-the-art accuracy on the TAU20 dataset when combined with BEATs underscores the framework's potential. Further, the inference complexity remains unchanged.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined problem and clear motivation:**  The paper clearly identifies a gap in existing ASC research and motivates the use of city-specific information.
    *   **Thorough experiments:** The authors conduct extensive experiments using several state-of-the-art models and multiple datasets. They also include ablation studies, sensitivity analysis, and visualizations to support their claims.
    *   **Consistent improvements:** The proposed City2Scene consistently improves the performance of various ASC models without increasing inference time.
    *   **Easy to apply:** City2Scene can be applied to different architectures, including CNNs and Transformers.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The focus solely on datasets where both scene and city labels are present limits broader applicability. The proposed framework leverages the availability of the city labels in training.
    *   **Dependence on City Label Quality:** The framework's performance hinges on the accuracy of the city labels. Any ambiguity or inaccuracies in these labels would negatively impact the results.
    *   **Limited analysis of city feature impact:** The paper provides a general overview of city feature benefits but does not delve into which specific city characteristics (e.g., transportation systems, architectural styles, cultural events) contribute most to improved ASC performance. The paper notes that scenes which are the most homogeneous sees the performance decrease.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research in ASC by highlighting the importance of location-specific information. The City2Scene framework can serve as a baseline for future work exploring the integration of location and environmental information into ASC systems. The work might also inspire research into methods for extracting city-specific features directly from audio data, even when explicit city labels are not available. The findings contribute to a more nuanced understanding of acoustic environments and their relationship to geographic context.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7**.

The paper presents a clearly defined approach and well-executed experiments with promising results. However, the framework relies on the availability and quality of city labels, and the analysis of specific city features is limited. This prevents it from achieving a higher score. However, the work offers a significant perspective change and the results are promising. The potential impact is high within the ASC community because city features have not been well-studied and the impact of City2Scene could lead to future research and analysis of city characteristics and integration of location in the ASC models.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 7/10

### Sparse Logit Sampling: Accelerating Knowledge Distillation in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16870v1)
- **Authors**: Anshumann, Mohd Abbas Zaidi, Akhil Kedia, Jinwoo Ahn, Taehwak Kwon, Kangwook Lee, Haejun Lee, Joohyung Lee
- **Abstract**: Knowledge distillation can be a cost-effective technique to distill knowledge in Large Language Models, if the teacher output logits can be pre-computed and cached. However, successfully applying this to pre-training remains largely unexplored. In this work, we prove that naive approaches for sparse knowledge distillation such as caching Top-K probabilities, while intuitive, provide biased estimates of teacher probability distribution to the student, resulting in suboptimal performance and calibration. We propose an importance-sampling-based method `Random Sampling Knowledge Distillation', which provides unbiased estimates, preserves the gradient in expectation, and requires storing significantly sparser logits. Our method enables faster training of student models with marginal overhead (<10%) compared to cross-entropy based training, while maintaining competitive performance compared to full distillation, across a range of model sizes from 300M to 3B.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Sparse Logit Sampling: Accelerating Knowledge Distillation in LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of efficient knowledge distillation (KD) for large language models (LLMs) in the pre-training setting. Storing the full logit distribution from a large teacher model is computationally infeasible. Naive approaches like caching the top-K probabilities lead to biased estimates and poor calibration, hindering student performance. The authors propose "Random Sampling Knowledge Distillation" (RS-KD), an importance-sampling-based method. RS-KD provides unbiased estimates of the teacher's distribution, preserves the gradient in expectation, and requires storing significantly fewer logits than alternative approaches. The authors demonstrate that RS-KD enables faster student model training with marginal overhead compared to cross-entropy training while maintaining competitive performance across a range of model sizes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The key novelty lies in the application of importance sampling to the problem of sparse knowledge distillation in the **pre-training** phase of LLMs. While the general idea of importance sampling is well-established, its adaptation to this specific scenario, with the rigorous theoretical backing provided (unbiased estimates, gradient preservation), constitutes a significant contribution. The paper clearly identifies the limitations of top-K approaches and provides a theoretically sound alternative that empirically outperforms them.
*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a practical and increasingly important problem in LLM development: efficient pre-training. Reducing the storage requirements for knowledge distillation opens up the possibility of training smaller, more efficient models on limited compute resources. The experiments are thorough and demonstrate that RS-KD can match the performance of full distillation while using significantly less storage. The improvements in calibration are also important, as well-calibrated models are more reliable and interpretable.  The large-scale experiments with the LLaMA-3-8B teacher are particularly compelling. The gains in downstream tasks are also significant, showcasing the effectiveness of RS-KD for pre-training.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Theoretical grounding:** The paper provides clear theoretical justification for the proposed method, demonstrating unbiasedness and gradient preservation.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The method is thoroughly evaluated on a range of model sizes and tasks, demonstrating its effectiveness in practice.
    *   **Practical relevance:** The paper addresses a real-world challenge in LLM development and provides a practical solution with tangible benefits (reduced storage, faster training).
    *   **Clear and well-written:** The paper is generally well-written and easy to follow, making the technical details accessible to a broader audience.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Novelty:** While the application of importance sampling is novel in *this specific context*, the underlying principle isn't new. There could have been exploration on how the method performs under different settings (different loss functions besides KL divergence).
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** The performance gains, while consistent and significant, might be considered incremental rather than revolutionary. The gains observed may be more significant in more high resource pretraining settings.
    *   **Evaluation Bias** The paper uses in-house or community models, which introduces evaluation bias by the authors. It can be significantly improved if a much larger model (100B parameter scale) is used instead.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a more efficient and practical approach to knowledge distillation for LLM pre-training. It could inspire further research on sparse KD methods and contribute to the development of more accessible and efficient LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper's novelty lies in its adaptation of a known technique to a specific LLM pre-training problem, and it offers significant practical benefits. Although the approach isn't groundbreaking in its theoretical contribution, its practical relevance and thorough validation warrant a strong score. The empirical results are convincing. I'm assigning a score of 7 out of 10 because the method presents a solid improvement over existing approaches, provides a theoretical analysis and has good practical implications.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Lie Detector: Unified Backdoor Detection via Cross-Examination Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16872v1)
- **Authors**: Xuan Wang, Siyuan Liang, Dongping Liao, Han Fang, Aishan Liu, Xiaochun Cao, Yu-liang Lu, Ee-Chien Chang, Xitong Gao
- **Abstract**: Institutions with limited data and computing resources often outsource model training to third-party providers in a semi-honest setting, assuming adherence to prescribed training protocols with pre-defined learning paradigm (e.g., supervised or semi-supervised learning). However, this practice can introduce severe security risks, as adversaries may poison the training data to embed backdoors into the resulting model. Existing detection approaches predominantly rely on statistical analyses, which often fail to maintain universally accurate detection accuracy across different learning paradigms. To address this challenge, we propose a unified backdoor detection framework in the semi-honest setting that exploits cross-examination of model inconsistencies between two independent service providers. Specifically, we integrate central kernel alignment to enable robust feature similarity measurements across different model architectures and learning paradigms, thereby facilitating precise recovery and identification of backdoor triggers. We further introduce backdoor fine-tuning sensitivity analysis to distinguish backdoor triggers from adversarial perturbations, substantially reducing false positives. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method achieves superior detection performance, improving accuracy by 5.4%, 1.6%, and 11.9% over SoTA baselines across supervised, semi-supervised, and autoregressive learning tasks, respectively. Notably, it is the first to effectively detect backdoors in multimodal large language models, further highlighting its broad applicability and advancing secure deep learning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Lie Detector," a novel backdoor detection framework designed for use in scenarios where model training is outsourced to potentially untrustworthy third-party providers. The framework leverages cross-examination of models trained independently by two different providers. It identifies backdoors by detecting inconsistencies in the models' outputs using Central Kernel Alignment (CKA) to measure feature similarity and a fine-tuning sensitivity analysis to distinguish true backdoors from benign model variations. The approach is presented as being more generalizable than existing methods, working across supervised, semi-supervised, and autoregressive learning paradigms, and is demonstrated to be effective on various datasets and attack types. The paper claims superior detection performance compared to state-of-the-art baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novelty of Approach:** The cross-examination framework is a relatively fresh perspective on backdoor detection, moving away from reliance on specific attack signatures or a clean reference model. Leveraging model inconsistency rather than absolute detection provides a degree of robustness against adaptive attacks.
    *   **Generality and Scope:** The claim of applicability across different learning paradigms (supervised, semi-supervised, autoregressive) is a significant potential advantage.  The experiments seem to support this claim.  The fact that it's purportedly the "first" to address backdoor detection in multimodal large language models is notable, expanding the scope of backdoor detection.
    *   **Technical Soundness:** The use of CKA and fine-tuning sensitivity analysis is technically well-motivated.  CKA's invariance properties are particularly useful for comparing models trained with slightly different architectures or objectives. The trigger optimization using output distribution loss and L1 regularization is a standard practice.
    *   **Experimental Evaluation:** The paper includes extensive experimental results, comparing against several baseline methods across various datasets and attack types. The gains reported over state-of-the-art are significant.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The threat model (semi-honest providers) and the limited resources on the user side are very relevant to real-world ML deployment scenarios.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Semi-Honest Assumption:** The semi-honest assumption is a key limitation. The approach assumes the providers *try* to hide the backdoor and *don't* try to sabotage the detection. A truly malicious provider could actively try to circumvent the cross-examination by coordinating their attacks, which will invalidate the assumption.
    *   **Operational Overhead:** Outsourcing the *same* task twice increases costs and operational complexity. Realistically, a user might only outsource one training task if budgets are tight. Although the increased security measures are useful, it remains an unproven solution.
    *   **Limited Details on Hyperparameter Optimization:** A key detail omitted is the hyperparameter tuning for *every* compared defense method. A simple comparison may not be accurate, if the compared defense method wasn't thoroughly optimized.
    *   **Dependency on the "Right" Layer:** The feature layer selection (layer 4) seems somewhat ad-hoc. While justified with an intuitive argument, it raises the question of how robust this choice is to different model architectures and datasets.
    *   **FLOPS not indicative of actual costs:** The table displaying "FLOPS" as a measure of computational cost doesn't accurately display the actual costs involved, which are more sensitive to hardware and implementation choices. This would be more accurate, if an actual measure was provided.

*   **Significance:** The paper offers a pragmatic approach to a critical security challenge in outsourced model training. If the approach proves robust in more complex and adaptive adversarial settings, it could have a real impact on how machine learning services are secured. The generality across different learning paradigms addresses a significant gap in existing research.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and technically sound approach to a relevant problem. The experimental results support the claimed advantages of the framework, particularly its generality. However, the semi-honest adversary assumption is a significant limitation that restricts the applicability of the method. There are also limited details that make the evaluation weaker than expected. Overall, I assess the paper as a strong and valuable contribution, but not a transformative one.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### MARS: A Multi-Agent Framework Incorporating Socratic Guidance for Automated Prompt Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16874v1)
- **Authors**: Jian Zhang, Zhangqi Wang, Haiping Zhu, Jun Liu, Qika Lin, Erik Cambria
- **Abstract**: The basic question-answering format of large language models involves inputting a prompt and receiving a response, and the quality of the prompt directly impacts the effectiveness of the response. Automated Prompt Optimization (APO) aims to break free from the cognitive biases of manually designed prompts and explores a broader design space for prompts. However, existing APO methods suffer from limited flexibility of fixed templates and inefficient search in prompt spaces as key issues. To this end, we propose a Multi-Agent framework Incorporating Socratic guidance (MARS), which utilizes multi-agent fusion technology for automatic planning, with gradual continuous optimization and evaluation. Specifically, MARS comprises seven agents, each with distinct functionalities, which autonomously use the Planner to devise an optimization path that ensures flexibility. Additionally, it employs a Teacher-Critic-Student Socratic dialogue pattern to iteratively optimize the prompts while conducting effective search. We conduct extensive experiments on various datasets to validate the effectiveness of our method, and perform additional analytical experiments to assess the model's advancement as well as the interpretability.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MARS: A Multi-Agent Framework Incorporating Socratic Guidance for Automated Prompt Optimization":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MARS, a multi-agent framework designed to improve Automated Prompt Optimization (APO) for Large Language Models (LLMs). MARS addresses limitations in existing APO methods, such as fixed templates and inefficient search, by using multiple specialized agents. A Planner agent autonomously devises optimization paths, and a Teacher-Critic-Student system employs a Socratic dialogue pattern to iteratively refine prompts. The paper presents experimental results on various general and domain-specific tasks, demonstrating MARS's effectiveness and interpretability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its multi-agent architecture for APO, especially the integration of a Socratic guidance dialogue pattern. While multi-agent systems and APO are not entirely new concepts, the combination within this specific context, particularly with the Socratic dialogue component, represents a contribution. The idea of using a Planner agent for dynamically generated optimization paths is also a valuable contribution. However, existing research has utilized chain-of-thought prompting and tree-of-thoughts approaches for more dynamic search through solutions, so the novelty is not a paradigm shift.

*   **Significance:** MARS demonstrates a significant improvement over existing APO methods. The empirical results presented across several datasets indicate a consistent performance boost. By combining automated planning with Socratic guidance, MARS also improves the interpretability of the optimization process, which is a welcome addition. The paper addresses a relevant and timely problem in the field of LLMs, as prompt engineering is a critical factor in their effectiveness. The paper has performed extensive experiments to validate its claims. The paper is well written and generally well organized.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Multi-Agent Architecture:** The use of specialized agents, each with a clearly defined role, facilitates a more structured and effective optimization process.
    *   **Socratic Guidance:** The Teacher-Critic-Student system promotes a more nuanced and interpretable approach to prompt refinement.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** Extensive experiments on both general and domain-specific tasks demonstrate the broad applicability of the MARS framework.
    *   **Improved Performance:** MARS outperforms several existing APO methods.
    *   **Interpretability:** Explicitly provides guidance and rationales for each stage of the generation process.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** The multi-agent architecture may add complexity to the system. The paper needs to demonstrate that the improvements justify the increase in complexity.
    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The framework relies heavily on LLMs as intelligent agents. While this is a strength, it also means that the performance of MARS is ultimately limited by the capabilities of the underlying LLMs. It's unclear if the benefit is merely stemming from "prompting the prompter."
    *   **Ablation studies:** While the paper contains an ablation study, it does not evaluate various alternative architectures for combining the agents to understand the importance of the current architecture.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of APO by promoting the use of multi-agent systems and more interpretable optimization techniques. The Socratic guidance dialogue pattern could inspire further research into interactive and human-in-the-loop approaches to prompt engineering.
    However, the potential is limited by the reliance of the method on the underlying LLM it is working to improve.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:**
    *   The experiments validate that the architecture improves outcomes.
    *   The detailed analysis of the approach validates the interpretability of the architecture.
    *   The modular design of the multi agent architecture means that it can potentially be transferred to new tasks and LLMs.
    *   The paper has demonstrated success with three different kinds of large language model, further validating its claims.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and useful multi-agent framework for APO, with a focus on interpretable Socratic guidance. It demonstrates improved performance over existing methods on a variety of tasks. While the underlying building blocks are not fundamentally novel, the combination and application within the specific context of APO, along with empirical validation, represent a worthwhile contribution. However, the gains do not represent a paradigm shift, and there are concerns with complexity and reliance on the LLM. Finally, the ablation studies can be expanded to demonstrate the efficacy of the current design over alternative agent pairings.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Federated Cross-Domain Click-Through Rate Prediction With Large Language Model Augmentation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16875v1)
- **Authors**: Jiangcheng Qin, Xueyuan Zhang, Baisong Liu, Jiangbo Qian, Yangyang Wang
- **Abstract**: Accurately predicting click-through rates (CTR) under stringent privacy constraints poses profound challenges, particularly when user-item interactions are sparse and fragmented across domains. Conventional cross-domain CTR (CCTR) methods frequently assume homogeneous feature spaces and rely on centralized data sharing, neglecting complex inter-domain discrepancies and the subtle trade-offs imposed by privacy-preserving protocols. Here, we present Federated Cross-Domain CTR Prediction with Large Language Model Augmentation (FedCCTR-LM), a federated framework engineered to address these limitations by synchronizing data augmentation, representation disentanglement, and adaptive privacy protection. Our approach integrates three core innovations. First, the Privacy-Preserving Augmentation Network (PrivAugNet) employs large language models to enrich user and item representations and expand interaction sequences, mitigating data sparsity and feature incompleteness. Second, the Independent Domain-Specific Transformer with Contrastive Learning (IDST-CL) module disentangles domain-specific and shared user preferences, employing intra-domain representation alignment (IDRA) and crossdomain representation disentanglement (CDRD) to refine the learned embeddings and enhance knowledge transfer across domains. Finally, the Adaptive Local Differential Privacy (AdaLDP) mechanism dynamically calibrates noise injection to achieve an optimal balance between rigorous privacy guarantees and predictive accuracy. Empirical evaluations on four real-world datasets demonstrate that FedCCTR-LM substantially outperforms existing baselines, offering robust, privacy-preserving, and generalizable cross-domain CTR prediction in heterogeneous, federated environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FedCCTR-LM, a federated learning framework for cross-domain click-through rate (CTR) prediction augmented with large language models (LLMs).  It addresses challenges such as non-I.I.D. data distributions, knowledge transfer barriers, and privacy-utility trade-offs in federated environments.  The framework consists of three core components: (1) PrivAugNet, a privacy-preserving augmentation network that uses LLMs to enrich user and item features and expand interaction sequences; (2) IDST-CL, an independent domain-specific transformer with contrastive learning that disentangles domain-specific and cross-domain preferences; and (3) AdaLDP, an adaptive local differential privacy mechanism that dynamically adjusts noise levels to balance privacy constraints and model utility. The authors demonstrate through experiments on multiple datasets that FedCCTR-LM outperforms existing baselines in terms of CTR prediction accuracy, privacy compliance, and scalability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addressing a Relevant Problem:**  The paper tackles a significant and practical problem in modern recommendation systems. CCTR under privacy constraints is crucial for real-world applications where data is distributed across multiple domains and user privacy is paramount.
    *   **Novelty:** The seamless integration of cross-domain CTR prediction within a federated environment with LLM augmentation is a novel approach. The combination of data augmentation, representation disentanglement, and adaptive privacy protection is well motivated.
    *   **Technical Soundness:** The proposed framework is technically sound and well-explained. The descriptions of PrivAugNet, IDST-CL, and AdaLDP are clear and detailed, making it easy to understand the implementation. The mathematical formulations of the loss functions and privacy mechanisms are rigorous.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The authors conduct extensive experiments on four benchmark datasets, demonstrating the effectiveness of FedCCTR-LM. The results show significant improvements over existing baselines in terms of NDCG and MRR. The ablation studies and sensitivity analyses provide valuable insights into the contributions of individual components and the effects of hyperparameter settings.
    *   **Comprehensive Ablation and Sensitivity Studies:** The detailed ablation studies and parameter sensitivity analyses solidify the claims about each component's contribution and the robustness of the framework to hyperparameter changes.
    *   **Clear and Well-Organized:** The paper is well-written, clearly structured, and easy to follow.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Complexity:** While the paper claims improved space complexity relative to FedDCSR, the practical computational overhead of fine-tuning or even just querying large language models within a federated setting is significant. The reliance on an API call doesn't fully address this as it still involves resource expenditure. A more detailed analysis of latency and cost associated with LLM calls is needed.
    *   **LLM Prompt Engineering Details:** While the paper mentions careful prompt design, the specific prompts used for the LLMs are only partially shown in Figure 4.  More detailed explanation of the prompt engineering process and the rationale behind the prompt design choices would strengthen the paper.  The sensitivity to prompt selection should also be addressed.
    *   **Potential for Negative Transfer:** The paper assumes that LLMs will always provide useful information for augmentation. While PrivAugNet mitigates this to some degree through contrastive learning, there is still the potential for introducing noise or biased information. The paper should discuss potential strategies for detecting and mitigating negative transfer effects.
    *   **Privacy Guarantees Scope:** The privacy guarantees provided are only local to the clients in each federated round. While AdaLDP dynamically adjust noise levels and this makes sense, it does not fully address the composition of the privacy costs over subsequent rounds and how they could be bounded at global level over multiple rounds.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of federated learning for recommendation systems. It introduces a novel framework that addresses the key challenges of CCTR in a privacy-preserving manner.
    *   The proposed techniques, such as PrivAugNet, IDST-CL, and AdaLDP, are valuable contributions that can be used in other applications as well.
    *   The paper provides a solid foundation for future research on federated recommendation systems with LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a valuable contribution to the field of federated learning and recommendation systems. It introduces a technically sound and well-evaluated framework for cross-domain CTR prediction that addresses key challenges in a privacy-preserving manner. While there are weaknesses in the computational analysis, detail on prompt selection, and potential negative transfer, the strengths of the paper outweigh its weaknesses. Therefore, the overall assessment is positive. The work exhibits significant novelty and provides a strong foundation for future research.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Improving the End-to-End Efficiency of Offline Inference for Multi-LLM Applications Based on Sampling and Simulation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16893v1)
- **Authors**: Jingzhi Fang, Yanyan Shen, Yue Wang, Lei Chen
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) have shown great success in many tasks, they are used in various applications. While a lot of works have focused on the efficiency of single-LLM application (e.g., offloading, request scheduling, parallelism strategy selection), multi-LLM applications receive less attention, particularly in offline inference scenarios. In this work, we aim to improve the offline end-to-end inference efficiency of multi-LLM applications in the single-node multi-GPU environment. The problem involves two key decisions: (1) determining which LLMs to run concurrently each time (we may not run all the models at the same time), and (2) selecting a parallelism strategy to use for each LLM. This problem is NP-hard. Naive solutions may not work well because the running time for a model to complete a set of requests depends on the request workload and the selected parallelism strategy, and they lack an accurate model of the running time. As the LLM output lengths are unknown before running, to estimate the model running time, we propose a sampling-then-simulation method which first estimates the output lengths by sampling from an empirical cumulative function we obtained from a large dataset in advance, and then simulates the LLM inference process accordingly. Based on the simulation, we estimate the per-iteration latencys to get the total latency. A greedy method is proposed to optimize the scheduling of the LLMs in the application across the GPUs. We then propose a framework SamuLLM which contains two phases: planning, which calls the greedy method for an application and running, which runs the application and dynamically adjust the model scheduling based on the runtime information. Experiments on 3 applications and a mixed application show that SamuLLM can achieve 1.0-2.4$\times$ end-to-end speedups compared to the competitors.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of improving the end-to-end efficiency of offline inference for multi-LLM applications in a single-node, multi-GPU environment. The core challenge lies in determining the optimal scheduling of LLMs across GPUs, considering both the subset of models to run concurrently and the parallelism strategy for each. The authors propose a "sampling-then-simulation" method to estimate the running time of a model given a set of requests and a model execution plan. This method uses empirical cumulative distribution functions (eCDFs) to estimate the output lengths of LLMs, which are unknown beforehand. Based on these estimates, the inference process is simulated, and a greedy algorithm is employed to optimize the scheduling of LLMs across GPUs. The authors then implement a framework called SamuLLM, which incorporates a planning phase (using the greedy method) and a running phase (with dynamic adjustment of the schedule based on runtime information). Experiments on various applications demonstrate that SamuLLM achieves speedups compared to baseline approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using a sampling-then-simulation approach to estimate LLM inference time, instead of attempting direct output length prediction, is a reasonable, though not groundbreaking, contribution. While leveraging eCDFs is not entirely novel in itself, its application to estimating LLM output lengths in the context of optimizing multi-LLM application scheduling is a sensible adaptation. The framework Samullm adds the value of the dynamic scheduling.

*   **Significance:** The problem the paper addresses is practically important. As LLMs become more prevalent, optimizing the efficiency of multi-LLM applications is crucial for resource utilization and cost savings. The performance improvements shown by SamuLLM, while not astronomical, are still meaningful, showing the advantage of a scheduling method that incorporates runtime information.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper provides a clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   The sampling-then-simulation method is a pragmatic solution to the challenge of unknown output lengths.
    *   The greedy scheduling algorithm, while not theoretically optimal, seems to work well in practice.
    *   The implementation of SamuLLM provides a concrete framework for deploying the proposed techniques.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of SamuLLM compared to baseline methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The sampling method relies on the assumption that output length distribution is largely independent of the specific request content. While the authors present evidence to support this claim, it may not hold true for all LLMs or applications.
    *   The greedy scheduling algorithm is not guaranteed to find the optimal solution. A more sophisticated optimization approach (e.g., dynamic programming or reinforcement learning) might yield better results, but would come at the cost of increased complexity.
    *   The experiments are limited to a single-node, multi-GPU environment. The techniques may not be directly applicable to distributed systems.
    *   The performance gains, while positive, are not dramatically large. It's possible that other optimization techniques, when combined with the authors’ approach, could result in better overall performance. The speedups, while present, aren't jaw-dropping enough to constitute a major leap forward.
    *   The dependency on the No Robots dataset for constructing eCDFs raises concerns about generalizability to other scenarios or LLMs where characteristics differ significantly.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence researchers to explore the use of simulation-based optimization techniques for LLM scheduling. The SamuLLM framework could serve as a starting point for developing more advanced scheduling systems.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a practical and well-engineered solution to an important problem. The sampling-then-simulation approach is a clever way to address the challenge of unknown output lengths. The experimental results demonstrate that the proposed techniques can improve the end-to-end efficiency of multi-LLM applications. While the novelty and significance of the paper are not groundbreaking, the work represents a solid engineering contribution that could have a tangible impact on the field. It doesn't introduce radical new concepts, but it provides a pragmatic solution to a relevant problem, backed by empirical validation.

### FAIT: Fault-Aware Fine-Tuning for Better Code Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16913v1)
- **Authors**: Lishui Fan, Zhongxin Liu, Haoye Wang, Lingfeng Bao, Xin Xia, Shanping Li
- **Abstract**: Modern instruction-tuned large language models (LLMs) have made remarkable progress in code generation. However, these LLMs fine-tuned with standard supervised fine-tuning (SFT) sometimes generate plausible-looking but functionally incorrect code variants. This issue likely stems from the limitation of standard SFT, which treats all tokens equally during optimization and fails to emphasize the error-sensitive segments-specific code differences between correct implementations and similar incorrect variants. To address this problem, we propose Fault-Aware Fine-Tuning (FAIT), a novel fine-tuning technique that enhances LLMs' code generation by (1) extracting multi-granularity (line/token-level) differences between correct and incorrect yet similar implementations to identify error-sensitive segments, and (2) dynamically prioritizing those segments during training via dynamic loss weighting. Through extensive experiments on seven LLMs across three widely-used benchmarks, our method achieves an average relative improvement of 6.9% on pass@1 with just one epoch of training, with some enhanced 6.7B LLMs outperforming closed-source models, e.g., GPT-3.5-Turbo. Furthermore, our fine-tuning technique demonstrates strong generalization with performance improvements ranging from 3.8% to 19.1% across diverse instruction-tuned LLMs, and our ablation studies confirm the contributions of different granularities of differences and loss function components.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "FAIT" (Fault-Aware Fine-Tuning), a novel fine-tuning technique for improving code generation in large language models (LLMs). FAIT addresses the issue of LLMs generating plausible-looking but functionally incorrect code by focusing on error-sensitive segments. It does this by (1) identifying these segments through multi-granularity difference analysis (line/token) between correct and similar, incorrect code implementations, and (2) dynamically prioritizing these segments during fine-tuning through a refined loss function with dynamic loss weighting.  The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of FAIT through experiments on several LLMs across multiple code generation benchmarks, showing significant improvements in pass@1.  The authors also present ablation studies that highlight the contributions of the different components of FAIT and demonstrate its generalizability and applicability to LLMs trained on both open and closed-source datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of focusing on error-sensitive segments during fine-tuning is novel and addresses a critical weakness of standard SFT approaches. Previous research primarily focused on instruction-tuning datasets and architectures, while FAIT directly targets the fine-grained details where errors tend to occur. The multi-granularity segment identification is a nice touch, as is the dynamic weighting scheme. While identifying crucial differences in code has been explored in debugging or program repair contexts, its application to LLM fine-tuning for code generation is a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The improvements in pass@1 achieved by FAIT are substantial, especially the ability of smaller, enhanced models to outperform larger, closed-source models like GPT-3.5-Turbo. This has significant practical implications, as it suggests FAIT can be used to create more reliable and efficient code generation systems. Demonstrating generalizability across diverse LLMs and datasets further enhances the practical impact. The ablation studies contribute to a better understanding of the key factors influencing code generation performance. Showing that it works with closed-source training data adds significant value, since most models are not transparent with their training data.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-designed approach with clearly defined steps (error-sensitive segment identification and dynamic importance reweighting).
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation on multiple LLMs and benchmarks.
    *   Detailed ablation studies to analyze component contributions.
    *   Demonstrated generalization ability.
    *   Direct comparison with state-of-the-art baselines (including closed-source models).

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The data generation process relies on a teacher model. While the authors manually inspected some outputs, there's still a potential for bias or inaccuracies in the generated incorrect code variants. Ideally, an automatic verification process for correctness of the error-sensitive segments would have been desired, however, it is unlikely to be feasible given that it would require more advanced testing tools than the evaluation benchmarks themselves.
    *   The hyperparameter sensitivity analysis focuses primarily on the weighting factor 'a'. Exploring the sensitivity to other hyperparameters (e.g., learning rate) could further strengthen the robustness of the findings. It is not clear whether the same *a* will work for other models and data.
    *   The comparison is limited to DPO. While DPO is a strong baseline, more comparisons can be included, but that would come at the expense of significantly expanding the scope of experiments.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of code generation by providing a more effective fine-tuning strategy. The idea of focusing on error-sensitive segments could be adapted to other tasks and modalities. It will likely encourage more research into fine-grained analysis of code differences and targeted training techniques.

*   **Rigorous Rationale for Score:** The score reflects a strong paper with a novel, well-executed approach that addresses a critical challenge in code generation. While there are some limitations, the strengths of the paper in terms of experimental evaluation, generalizability, and potential impact outweigh those limitations. The paper offers a practical and effective technique for improving LLMs' code generation abilities.

**Score: 8.5**
